Title: Recursive-Head Geometry and Order-Free Efficient Inference in Finite-State Nested Markov Models

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.17939v1

Published Time: Thu, 17 Sep 2026 00:22:38 GMT

Number of Pages: 101

Markdown Content:
RECURSIVE-HEAD GEOMETRY AND ORDER-FREE EFFICIENT INFERENCE IN FINITE-STATE NESTED MARKOV MODELS 

BY HAOYU WEI 1,a  

> 1Department of Economics, University of California, San Diego, ah8wei@ucsd.edu

Exploiting the equality restrictions that nested Markov models encode requires their tangent-space geometry. For strictly positive finite-state mod-els on arbitrary acyclic directed mixed graphs (ADMGs), we differentiate the recursive-head chart and prove that the range of its score map is the full tangent space. Intrinsic-set coordinate blocks form an algebraic direct sum, blocks of distinct districts are orthogonal, and the resulting Gram projection needs neither mb-shieldedness nor a district order. Exact counterexamples show that observational centering and kernel normalization alone do not cer-tify tangency. For the node, complete-source edge, and compatible path-specific inter-vention targets considered here, boundary substitution yields a normalized configured active law and an order-free canonical-gradient formula, extended to finite mixtures by independent source redraws. A coherent one-step esti-mator with an exact remainder identity and a model-valid chart-flow targeted maximum likelihood estimator are efficient under stated local conditions. On a narrower source-isolated fixed-node subclass, a sequential estimator has an exact transition-factorized drift and up to 2m nuisance-correctness regimes over m active-district transitions; its all-correct influence function projects onto the canonical gradient, and an exact rational law exhibits a strict vari-ance gap. These results separate order-free efficiency on arbitrary finite-state ADMGs from multiple robustness of a narrower construction.

1. Introduction. Nested Markov models encode equality constraints induced by latent-variable structure that are invisible to ordinary conditional-independence models [13]. Such constraints matter for inference even when the causal functional of interest is already identi-fied: an estimator that ignores an equality restriction satisfied by the data-generating law can forgo precision, because the efficiency bound of the restricted model is never larger, and can be strictly smaller, than that of the model with the restriction dropped. Exploiting this gain requires the tangent space of the restricted model and the projection that yields the target’s ORDER-FREE INFERENCE IN NESTED MARKOV MODELS 3

canonical gradient. This paper supplies that geometry for strictly positive finite-state nested Markov models on arbitrary ADMGs, without mb-shieldedness or an ordering of districts, and develops the efficient and robust estimators it supports. Equality constraints of latent-variable margins beyond conditional independence were noted in [14, 26], and the c-component, or district, factorization for causal identification was developed in [22]. The Markov properties of ADMGs were established in [12], and the discrete ordinary Markov model was parameterized in [6]. Richardson, Evans, Robins and Shpitser [13] defined the nested Markov model through fixing, proved that margins of DAG models lie in it, and characterized identifiable node interventions by fixing; the dis-crete latent-variable model is algebraically equivalent to the corresponding nested Markov model and has the same dimension [5]. Evans and Richardson [7] proved that the recursive-head parameterization is a smooth identifiable chart of the finite-state nested model, which is therefore a curved exponential family; that chart is the object differentiated here. Iden-tification of node interventions under latent confounding is complete [17, 18]; node, edge and path interventions were organized into a hierarchy in [20], and graphical conditions for experimental identification of path-specific effects were given in [1]. The complete-source edge and path-specific classes of Section 5 are identified under the conditions of [16, 19]; that section applies those results under their stated hypotheses and does not enlarge them. On the estimation side, semiparametric efficiency theory and doubly robust estimation [2, 4, 15, 23] were extended to mediation and path-specific functionals in [8, 10, 21, 27], with estimator-specific combinations of correctly specified nuisance components. The effi-ciency calculations for these mediation functionals use unrestricted observed-data models and do not incorporate additional nested Markov restrictions. Multiple robustness in factor-ized likelihood models was characterized in [11]. Closest to the present work, Bhattacharya, Nabi and Shpitser [3] obtain doubly robust augmented inverse probability weighted esti-mators under primal fixability, without mb-shieldedness; their tangent-space decomposition and corresponding efficiency results assume mb-shieldedness. Targeted maximum likelihood estimation [25] uses locally least favorable updates; for a scalar target, the universal least fa-vorable submodel [24] follows the canonical gradient along the path. The present work sup-plies the tangent-space geometry of the nested model on an arbitrary finite-state ADMG—an order-free orthogonal decomposition by native districts with an explicit projection—and a targeting path that stays inside the model, constructed in recursive-head coordinates. The setting is finite-state and interior throughout: every variable has a fixed finite state space, and the operative statistical model is the strictly positive part N+(G) of the nested Markov model N (G), not the latent-margin model; the arbitrary-cardinality theorems do not rely on the binary worked examples. On this support the recursive-head parameterization of [7] is a smooth identifiable chart and the model is a curved exponential family [7, Corol-lary 5.6], so regular finite-dimensional inference is available in principle. What that fact does not supply is the geometry needed to use it on a graph: which functions of the data are model scores, how the scores attached to different parts of the graph relate to one another, and how an intervention target and its canonical gradient are expressed in the chart. The difficulty is the identification of the correct score subspaces, not dimension counting. A function centered under an observational conditional distribution need not be centered under a fixed intrinsic kernel, and a normalized tilt of a district kernel need not preserve the internal nested con-straints of that district; Section 4 exhibits exact failures of both on a non-mb-shielded graph. We therefore differentiate the forward recursive-head chart itself: the derivative of the chart map in a coordinate direction, divided pointwise by the mass function at that chart point, is a model-valid score by construction, and the coordinate blocks indexed by intrinsic sets give score blocks whose position relative to one another in L2(P ) is the object of study. Section 2 defines this chart score JP and its blocks SC (P ).4

Contributions. The parameterization is due to [7]; the contributions of this paper are graph-specific consequences of its differential geometry, each stated here through its principal re-sult. (i) Native-district separation without an order (Theorem 3.8). At every strictly positive law the tangent space is the L2(P )-orthogonal direct sum of the score spaces of the native dis-tricts, on every finite-state ADMG, without mb-shieldedness, a district order, or acyclicity of the district quotient. The metric projection onto the tangent space is therefore a sum of districtwise Gram projections (Proposition 3.10), which is what makes an explicit order-free efficient projection possible. (ii) Configured active-chart selection (Theorem 5.5). For the node, complete-source edge, and compatible path-specific targets of classes (I1)–(I3) in Section 5, substituting the boundary values of the intervention selects existing tail-indexed coordinates and reassem-bles a normalized nested Markov law of the active graph, throughout N+(G). The canoni-cal gradient of the identified target is then a direct chart derivative solved district by district (Theorem 5.8), with no vertex order and no change-of-measure argument; finite mixtures defined by independent source redraws are covered by a product rule (Theorem 5.12). (iii) Efficient one-step estimation and chart targeting (Theorems 6.7 and 6.16). For an initial fit that is not an interior likelihood solution, a coherent one-step estimator built at a single fitted chart law has an exact remainder identity and is efficient under an op(n−1/4) chart rate. Under the local conditions of Theorem 6.16, a least-favorable flow in recursive-head coordinates moves an initial model law to an empirical efficient-influence-function root on an event of probability tending to one; the resulting targeted estimator preserves sub-stitution and range on every sample through its prespecified fallback and is asymptotically equivalent to the same-sample one-step estimator. (iv) A construction-specific efficiency–robustness comparison (Theorems 7.6 and 7.7). On the narrower source-isolated fixed-node subclass of Section 7, a full-history sequential estimator has an exact transition-factorized drift and is consistent on up to 2m nuisance-correctness regimes, m being the number of active-district transitions, but its all-correct influence function is generally not canonical: its projection onto the tangent space is the order-free canonical gradient, and an exact law exhibits a strict variance gap (Proposi-tion 7.8). The comparison is between two constructions, not an impossibility theorem, and no union-model result is claimed for the full range of interventions in (I1)–(I3). 

Organization. Section 2 fixes notation and the finite-state chart; Section 3 proves the tangent-space theorem, the district decomposition, and the Gram projection; Section 4 records the exact failure of the larger raw spaces. Section 5 states the causal identification in-put and proves the normalized active chart, its canonical gradient, and the independent-draw mixture extension. Section 6 develops the coherent one-step estimator and the chart-flow tar-geted estimator. Section 7 gives the restricted sequential alternative, its exact union drift, and the variance comparison, which Section 8 illustrates at one law; Section 9 discusses what an extension beyond fixed finite support would require. Appendix A and Appendix B are included below, with their sections, results, equations, figures and tables all numbered con-tinuously with the article: proofs of the results of Sections 2–7 are collected in Sections A.3 and A.4, with the result-by-result scope summary in Section A.1 and the notation index in Section A.2, and Appendix B keeps the exact computational verification separate from the general proofs. 

2. Finite-state nested Markov setup. Let G = ( V, E ) be an acyclic directed mixed graph on a finite vertex set V , and let XV = ( Xv : v ∈ V ) take values in XV = ×v∈V Xv ,where every Xv is finite and has at least two elements .ORDER-FREE INFERENCE IN NESTED MARKOV MODELS 5

A conditional ADMG (CADMG) H = ( R, W, E H) [7, Definition 2.1] has disjoint sets 

R of random vertices and W of fixed vertices. Its directed edges belong to (R ∪ W ) × R,its bidirected edges have both endpoints in R, and its directed part is acyclic; in particular, no edge has an arrowhead at a fixed vertex. Under the standing convention immediately following Definition 2.1 of [7], each displayed fixed vertex also has at least one child in 

R; Richardson-style fixing graphs may temporarily retain childless fixed vertices, which the reduction defined below removes. A probability kernel for XR given XW is a nonnegative array qR(xR | xW ) satisfying

qR(xR | xW ) = 1 for every xW ∈ XW .

An ADMG is the special case W = ∅.For a law P , write p for its probability mass function and P f = E P f . Probabilities of events are written with an upright P: P( ·) when the law is generic or clear from the context, and PQ(·) under a named law Q, so that PP (XA = xA) = P

Pθ introduced below we abbreviate PPθ to Pθ . Italic P , P0, Pθ and P I denote laws, lower-case p and q denote mass functions and kernels, and Pn denotes an empirical measure. We write N (G) for the finite-state nested Markov model of [5, Definition 3.1] and define its strictly positive part by 

N+(G) := {P ∈ N (G) : p(xV ) > 0 for every xV ∈ XV }.

We use the CADMG recursive factorization of [7, Definition 3.3] for coordinate synthesis. Proposition 2.1 below establishes the bridge in the direction this paper uses: every law in 

N+(G) is nested Markov in the sense of [13, Definitions 27–28, Proposition 29 and Theo-rem 38], so the fixing calculus of that source applies to such laws throughout — the recursive formulation constructs the forward chart, and the fixing formulation identifies its reachable kernels. The converse direction is neither claimed nor used. Write D(G) for the districts and I(G) for the intrinsic sets [see Definition 3 and Defini-tion 33 in 13]; the sans-serif symbol I is reserved for interventions. For A ⊆ V , write GA

for the ordinary vertex-induced ADMG on A: its vertex set is A, and it retains exactly those directed and bidirected edges of G whose endpoints both lie in A. Applied to a CADMG the same construction additionally preserves each vertex’s random or fixed status [13]; for an ADMG that clause is vacuous, since every vertex is random. Square brackets are reserved throughout this paper for the reduced CADMG of [7, Definition 2.8], so G[C] never abbrevi-ates an unreduced fixing graph. Following [7, Definition 2.2], graphical relations are applied disjunctively to sets; in particular, pa H(A) := S 

> a∈A

pa H(a).For C ∈ I (G), its recursive head and tail are 

H(C) := C \ pa G (C), T (C) := pa G (C).

Thus, H(C) = sterile G (C) is the sterile subset of C—the set of sink nodes in the induced subgraph on C—and T (C) ∩ C = C \ H(C). Recursive heads and intrinsic sets are in one-to-one correspondence [7, Definition 4.1, Lemma 4.6, and the following remark]. Lemma A.2 of the Supplementary Material shows that the intrinsic sets of Definition 33 of [13] are exactly those of that source, so the correspondence applies to I(G) as defined here. Every intrinsic set is bidirected-connected and therefore lies in a unique native district of G. More generally, the districts of G are its bidirected-connected components, so every nonempty bidirected-connected S ⊆ V lies in exactly one of them; write δ(S) for that district. Besides every intrinsic set, this covers every district of a vertex-induced subgraph GA, because GA retains exactly the bidirected edges of G with both endpoints in A, so a bidirected path witnessing connectedness in GA is one in G.6

For D ∈ D (G), write dD(G) for the district CADMG of [7, Definition 2.5], whose random vertices are D and whose fixed vertices are pa G (D) \ D. It retains every edge of G with an arrowhead at a vertex in D. The forward map defined in (2.4) applies verbatim to a CADMG, conditionally on each value of its fixed vertices; we write that map as ΦdD (G).More generally, for C ⊆ V , let G[C] denote the reduced CADMG of [7, Definition 2.8]: its random vertices are C, its fixed vertices are pa G (C) \ C, its bidirected edges are those with both endpoints in C, and its directed edges run from C ∪ pa G (C) into C. Equivalently, it is the subgraph containing precisely the edges whose arrowheads are all in C. When C is intrinsic, this is the reduced CADMG used for its intrinsic kernel. A random vertex v ∈ R of a CADMG H = ( R, W, E H) is fixable when no other vertex of its district is a directed descendant of it, de H(v) ∩ dis H(v) = {v}; write F(H) for the set of fixable vertices. Fixing a fixable v deletes every edge with an arrowhead at v and makes v a fixed vertex. For a strictly positive kernel q that is Markov with respect to H,the kernel operation divides by the conditional of Xv given its Markov blanket mb H(v) := 

{dis H(v) ∪ pa H(dis H(v)) } \ { v} in the current graph: 

ϕv(q; H)( xR\{ v} | xW ∪{ v}) := q(xR | xW )

q(xv | xmb H(v)) ,

where the divisor is q(xv | xmb H(v)∩R, x W ), formed by marginalizing and conditionalizing q

over random variables; the Markov property makes it independent of the fixed arguments in-dexed by W \mb H(v), which the displayed shorthand suppresses [13, Definitions 17 and 19]. A fixing sequence is valid when each vertex is fixable in the graph current at its turn, iterated fixing ϕω is the composition along a valid sequence ω, and a set is reachable when some valid sequence produces it as the random set [13, Definition 26]; a set is intrinsic 

when it is in addition a district in a reachable CADMG [13, Definition 33]. Reachability alone is not sufficient — in a → b with a third isolated vertex, {a, b } is reachable but not intrinsic — whereas in a directed acyclic graph every district is a singleton, so the intrinsic sets are exactly the singletons {v}, with T ({v}) = pa G (v), and the chart below reduces to the ordinary conditional-probability parameterization. Every intrinsic C is reachable as the entire random set of a terminal CADMG whose single district is C (Section A.3.3 of the Supplementary Material). Intrinsicness is what licenses the intrinsic kernel qC on G[C] and the coordinate block indexed by H(C) and T (C): those two sets are defined by the displayed set operations for any C, but only for intrinsic C do they index a block of the chart. For a reachable random set R with raw fixing pair ( eHR, eqR), define the reduction 

red( eHR, eqR) := ( HR, q R)

by deleting the childless — hence isolated — fixed vertices of eHR and suppressing the cor-responding kernel arguments; the operation is well defined whenever eqR is constant in those arguments. This is the general operator red S , the subscript naming the surviving random set; 

red C , red D , red ∆ and the stagewise red Rj below are its instances. The reduction is pair-valued; red R(eqR) denotes its kernel component, the raw graph being understood. PROPOSITION 2.1 (Compatibility of the recursive and fixing formulations). Let G be an ADMG on a finite V with every Xv finite, and let p > 0 be a mass function on XV with law P ; for V = ∅, every clause is read through the empty reduced-CADMG convention of Appendix A.3 of the Supplementary Material, under which all three descriptions in (i) hold trivially. (i) The following are equivalent: (a) P ∈ N (G), the model of [5, Definition 3.1]; (b) p is representable in the head–tail parameterization of that source (§3.2, opening paragraph); 

(c) p recursively factorizes according to G in the sense of [7, Definition 3.3]. Here (b) ⇐⇒ 

(c) is [7, Theorem 5.4], whose general finite-state form is Proposition 2.5 (i) .

(ii) If the equivalent conditions in (i) hold, then P satisfies the nested Markov property of [13, Definition 27]. Consequently, for every reachable random set R, the raw fixing pair 

(ϕω(G), ϕ ω(p; G)) is the same for every valid sequence ω reaching R [13, Theorem 31]; the raw kernel eqR := ϕω(p; G) is strictly positive, and it is constant in the arguments indexed by the childless fixed vertices of ϕω(G) [13, Corollary 32]; and its reduction 

qR := red R(eqR) is a strictly positive kernel that recursively factorizes according to the reduced CADMG HR, hence lies in Pc(HR) by Lemma A.3. 

(iii) If the equivalent conditions in (i) hold, then for every reduced reachable pair (H0, q R0 )

reached by a valid sequence and every C ∈ I (H0), the kernel rC recursively derived from 

(H0, q R0 ) as in Proposition 2.5 (iii) equals the intrinsic kernel qC of (2.1) below, for every admissible derivation. 

Clause (i) is by citation, as stated. Clauses (ii)–(iii), the transfer of Markov membership across constant extension by childless fixed vertices, and the derivation-level form of (iii) are stated in full and proved in Section A.3.3 of the Supplementary Material (Lemma A.3 and Proposition A.4), by induction along the fixing sequence. The intrinsic kernel of C ∈ I (G) is the reduction of its fixing kernel. (Section A.3.3 of the Supplementary Material). REMARK 2.2 (Cellwise polynomial and multiaffine structure). For fixed (xR, x W ), say-ing that ΦH is polynomial means that 

η 7 −→ ΦH(η)( xR | xW )

is a real polynomial in the finitely many tagged coordinates of UH. More strongly, every monomial in (2.4) is square-free. Indeed, each partition PH(A) contains distinct recursive heads; the head–intrinsic-set bijection and the tagged coordinate construction then place the corresponding factors in distinct coordinate blocks. Thus no coordinate can occur twice in a partition product. Finite summation preserves this property, so every cell is affine in each coordinate separately. For i ∈ A H, let ei ∈ UH be the corresponding coordinate vector. For every η ∈ UH and t ∈

R, the polynomial map is Fréchet differentiable; write DΦH,η for its differential. Multiaffinity gives the array identity 

ΦH(η + te i) = Φ H(η) + t{ΦH(η + ei) − ΦH(η)}, DΦH,η [ei] = Φ H(η + ei) − ΦH(η).

These are algebraic identities on the whole ambient coordinate space; the synthesized array need not be nonnegative at either η or η + ei.10

PROPOSITION 2.5 (Finite-state recursive-head parameterization). Let H = ( R, W, E H)

be a reduced finite-state CADMG, with a corner state fixed for each random vertex, and let 

ΦH be given by (2.4) .

(i) Representation. A strictly positive probability kernel qR(· | · ) recursively factorizes ac-cording to H if and only if qR = Φ H(η) for some η ∈ UH.

(ii) Uniqueness. If qR is strictly positive and ΦH(η) = Φ H(η′) = qR, then η = η′.

(iii) Identification. For a strictly positive recursively factorizing qR, let rC be the unique recursively derived kernel on an intrinsic set C, and put H = H(C) and T = T (C). The unique coordinate vector ΘH(qR) ∈ UH is given by 

(2.7) {ΘH(qR)}C (aH | xT ) = rC (aH , x C\H | xT \C )

X 

> zH∈XH

rC (zH , x C\H | xT \C ) .

If qR is a reachable kernel obtained by fixing a strictly positive nested Markov law on an ADMG, then, by Proposition 2.1(iii), the recursively derived rC equals the corresponding intrinsic fixing kernel qC in (2.1) ; in that setting (2.7) is exactly (2.3) .

(iv) Ambient smooth recovery. Fix one recursive derivation scheme for every intrinsic set and reference values for any arguments that the scheme discards on the model. On the open positive orthant 

OH := (0 , ∞)XR×XW ,

the same finite sequence of marginalizations, conditionalizations and coordinate ratios defines a rational, hence C∞, map eΘH : OH → UH. Its restriction to strictly positive recursively factorizing kernels is ΘH. Off the model, its value may depend on the chosen derivation scheme. 

(v) Inverse identities. The two inverse identities are 

(2.8) ΦH{ΘH(qR)} = qR, eΘH{ΦH(η)} = η. 

The first identity holds for every strictly positive recursively factorizing kernel; the second holds whenever ΦH(η) is a strictly positive probability kernel. 

Define its strict-positivity region by 

ΩG := {θ ∈ UG : Φ G (θ)( xV ) > 0 for every xV ∈ XV }.ORDER-FREE INFERENCE IN NESTED MARKOV MODELS 11 

For θ ∈ ΩG , Corollary 2.4 and Proposition 2.5 show that pθ := Φ G (θ) is a strictly positive recursively factorizing mass function — hence, by Proposition 2.1(i), a nested Markov one; write Pθ for its law. Theorem 3.1 will package the resulting bijection and its smooth dif-ferential consequences: under the probability-mass-function identification, θ 7 → Pθ maps ΩG

2.3. Scores and tangent spaces. Fix P ∈ N +(G) with mass function p, and equip func-tions on XV with 

⟨f, g ⟩P := E P {f (XV )g(XV )}, ∥f ∥P, 2 := ⟨f, f ⟩1/2 

> P

.

Write L2(P ) for the resulting finite-dimensional Hilbert space and L02(P ) for its subspace of mean-zero functions. Following the standard pathwise convention [9, Section 18.1], the tangent space TP N (G) is the L2(P )-closure of the linear span of scores of regular two-sided paths {Pt : |t| < ϵ } ⊆ N (G) satisfying P0 = P , a regular path t 7 → pt being cellwise differentiable at zero with score s(xV ) = ˙ p0(xV )/p (xV ). On a finite support with p > 0,cellwise differentiability at zero is equivalent to differentiability in quadratic mean at zero with the same score, since each cell’s square root is then differentiable and the quadratic-mean remainder is a finite sum of cellwise remainders; cellwise continuity places every such path inside N+(G) after its parameter interval is shortened if necessary, so N (G) and N+(G)

have the same tangent space at P , and that space is finite-dimensional, so the closure adds no further directions. Put θ0 = Θ G (P ). Remark 2.2 shows cellwise that ΦG is polynomial, hence Fréchet dif-ferentiable, between finite-dimensional spaces. Throughout, D denotes the differential of a map between finite-dimensional spaces and Df [u] its value in the direction u, which is the ordinary directional derivative; the letter D is reserved for districts, and DP in Remark 3.11 is multiplication by p, not a differential. For u ∈ UG ,

DΦG,θ 0 [u] := ddt ΦG (θ0 + tu )

> t=0

Define

p(xV ) .

Pointwise division by p turns the derivative of the cell probabilities into a log-density derivative. Thus JP u is the score of the coordinate path t 7 → ΦG (θ0 + tu ) whenever that line remains in ΩG ; Theorem 3.1 supplies a two-sided interval for every u. Using the fixed identi-fication UG ∼= Rd, regard the canonical map ιC : UC → UG above as coordinate insertion into 

Rd. For a linear map A, ran( A) denotes its range. Set 

JC,P := JP ιC , SC (P ) := ran( JC,P ).

Thus JC,P inserts a perturbation in the C-tagged coordinate block and maps it to its model-valid score, while SC (P ) collects all such scores. These are the operative intrinsic-set score spaces. Membership in them, and in TP N (G), is decided by the chart and is a different prop-erty from observational centering, the vanishing of a conditional mean given an observational conditioning set; the two are kept apart throughout, and Section 4 exhibits an observationally centered direction that is not a model score. 12

REMARK 3.7 (Local compatibility is not arbitrary kernel tilting). Equation (3.6) permits independent selection, from the district domains, of district factors lying in Q+(dD(G)) = ΦdD (G)(Ω dD (G)). It does not permit independently chosen arbitrary normalized district ker-nels: each rD,θ [D] must lie in that chart image and retain all internal nested constraints. In general, ΩdD (G) is variation dependent and need not factor over the individual intrinsic-set blocks within D. Openness nevertheless gives a Euclidean ball at every interior point, and hence a rectangle for any finite collection of intrinsic- or district-block directions. 3.2. Cross-district orthogonality.

4. Exact non-mb-shielded counterexample. Consider the binary ADMG whose di-rected and bidirected edges are (4.1) directed: W → Z, B → Z, Z → Y, 

bidirected: A ↔ B, A ↔ Z, B ↔ Y. 

Its native districts are {W } and ∆ = {A, B, Z, Y }, with quotient {W } → ∆; see Figure 1. With the Markov blanket mb G (v) of Section 2, an ADMG is mb-shielded when any two 16      

> WZYAB
> FIG 1. The binary ADMG of (4.1) . Solid arrows are directed edges; dashed double arrows are bidirected edges.

vertices are adjacent whenever either belongs to the other’s Markov blanket [3, Theorem 2]. The displayed graph is not mb-shielded: W and A are nonadjacent although W ∈ mb G (A) = 

{B, Z, Y, W }.On strictly positive laws, its nested Markov model imposes no restriction beyond ordinary conditional independence. The reachable {B, Y } kernel qBY,p (b, y | z, w ) = p(b | w) p(y |

b, z, w ), obtained by fixing A, Z and W , is invariant in w at every model law — equivalently 

FBY (p) = 0 in (B.3) of the Supplementary Material — and that invariance holds exactly when B ⊥⊥ W and Y ⊥⊥ W | (B, Z ); Appendix B shows that N+(G) is the strictly posi-tive ordinary Markov model {p > 0 : W ⊥⊥ (A, B ), Y ⊥⊥ W | (B, Z )} of G, in which the joint independence W ⊥⊥ (A, B ) is part of the model and is not implied by the kernel invari-ance. No DAG on V represents this model (Section B.1). Adding the single arrow W → B

produces a genuine Verma restriction; see Remark 7.10. The example matters because G is not mb-shielded: the mb-shielded decomposition theo-rem [3, Theorem 2] does not apply to it, whereas Theorem 3.8 does, and the observationally centered direction h defined next is not a model score. h(XV ) := XY − EP (XY | XZ ).

Intrinsic-kernel versus observational centering. The intrinsic kernel of the single-ton set {Y } is q{Y }(y | z), obtained by fixing the other four vertices, for instance in the valid order A, Z, W, B . At the terminal stage, where Y is the only random vertex, this kernel is also the fixing divisor; the divisor for fixing Y in the original graph is instead p(y | a, b, z, w ). At 

P , q{Y }(0 | 0) = 0 .55 and PP (Y = 0 | Z = 0) = 0 .718 . Both values follow from (B.1) of the Supplementary Material, as recorded in Section B.1. Thus, although Y is a directed sink and can be placed last in a topological order, its intrinsic kernel differs from the observational con-ditional distribution. In particular, the observationally centered direction h = Y − EP (Y | Z)

satisfies P1

Therefore dim K∆(P ) = 2(2 4 − 1) = 30 .On the other hand, G has nine intrinsic sets, one of which is {W }; the eight in the ∆ block, with their recursive heads, tails and binary block dimensions dC = 2 |T (C)|, are tabulated in Section B.1 of the Supplementary Material, and their dimensions sum to 23 . The restriction W ⊥⊥ (A, B ) contributes (2 − 1)(4 − 1) = 3 scalar constraints, and Y ⊥⊥

W | (B, Z ) contributes 4(2 − 1)(2 − 1) = 4 . Their differentials are independent on the sim-plex tangent at every positive model law: the four conditional constraints have independent differentials along perturbations of p(y | w, a, b, z ), which leave the (W, A, B ) margin fixed, and the three marginal constraints have independent differentials along perturbations of that margin. Thus 31 − 7 = 24 , the chart dimension recorded in Section B.1 of the Supplementary Material. Hence kernel normalization is necessary for a valid district path but is not sufficient: the quotient in (4.4) contains seven linearly independent normalized-kernel directions that are ex-cluded by the linearized Markov restrictions of G at P , here the seven ordinary-independence constraints W ⊥⊥ (A, B ) and Y ⊥⊥ W | (B, Z ). This does not by itself rule out every al-ternative factorized nuisance representation; see the discussion closing Section 6. This is a structural statement, not a feature of either rational law used below. 18 

4.3. What is structural and what is evidence. The containment and codimension-seven conclusion above hold at every P ∈ N +(G). The exact calculations at the primary law P

and at the second active-latent law P ′ of (B.2) of the Supplementary Material, recorded in Section B.1, are finite-law corroborations of Theorems 3.1 and 3.8, not proofs for arbitrary graphs or arbitrary positive laws; properties holding at one law but not the other are not used in the theory. 4.4. Cyclic district quotients. Let Gcyc be the binary ADMG on {A, A ′, B, B ′} with bidi-rected edges A ↔ A′ and B ↔ B′ and directed edges A → B and B′ → A′: its directed vertex graph is acyclic, but its two native districts form a directed two-cycle in the district quotient (Section B.2 of the Supplementary Material: the edge-list display (B.5), Figure 2, and the detailed development). At the strictly positive rational law Pcyc of (B.9), all 25 entries of the cross-district Gram block vanish exactly (B.6), as Theorem 3.8 requires, whereas the two ob-servationally centered directions of (B.7) have a nonzero inner product and strictly positive exact squared projection residuals (B.8); Remark 3.9 records the head-family comparison at the same law. The law is thus a positive control for Theorem 3.8 and a negative control for observational centering as a tangency criterion. 4.5. A structurally misleading pairwise diagnostic. Let G3 be the six-node control of (B.10), whose three bidirected pairs form a directed three-cycle in the district quotient al-though the directed vertex graph is acyclic (Section B.3 of the Supplementary Material: Fig-ure 3 and the detailed development). For the raw residuals of (B.11), the pairwise orthogo-nality (B.12) is structural : it holds at every P ∈ N +(G3), through the m-separation global Markov property. Non-tangency is law-specific : at the rational law P3 of (B.15), the three exact squared projection residuals (B.13) are strictly positive and the third moment (B.14) is nonzero. The projection residuals, not the third moment, certify the non-tangency claims: the graph-implied conditional independences together with observational centering force the displayed pairwise inner products to vanish even though the directions are not model scores. 

5. A chart-level intervention bridge. This section separates the causal identification input, which is used only at the true causal law, from the statistical geometry of Sections 2–3: the same fixing functional is defined on all of N+(G) and shown to be a smooth normalized chart functional there. Remark 5.7 states the distinction precisely. 5.1. The causal identification input. Let Y ⊆ V be the outcome set. For a fixed node intervention, let AI be the intervened vertices. For a fixed edge intervention α, let AI be the sources of its intervened arrows; for a path-specific intervention of class (I3) below, let it be the treatment-source set. Assume throughout that Y ∩ AI = ∅. We call α complete-source if, once an arrow out of a ∈ AI is included, every directed arrow out of a is included in α. This is the convention under which Theorem 1 of [19] is stated. Put (5.1) RI := an GV \AI (Y ), HI := GRI .

For a system-wide response, Y = V \ AI and hence RI = V \ AI.The intervention classes considered are the following; the common configuration condi-tions above apply to all of them. (I1) A fixed-value node intervention. (I2) A complete-source fixed-value edge intervention: every source a ∈ AI assigns one com-mon value aa,D ∈ Xa to all arrows from a into any one district D ∈ D (HI), and values may differ across active districts. Its causal model is stated in (H2) and its identification hypotheses in (H3)–(H4). ORDER-FREE INFERENCE IN NESTED MARKOV MODELS 19 

(I3) A path-specific intervention, by one of two routes. 

Direct route. A standard path-specific intervention along specified proper causal paths from a treatment set to Y , with two fixed treatment-value vectors, treated under the causal model of (H2) and the route-specific hypotheses of (H3)–(H4); these are the cases covered directly by [16, Theorems 3–4]. 

Reduced route. A path intervention that an independently justified edge-consistent re-duction turns into an intervention satisfying every condition in (I2), which is then treated under every (I2) hypothesis; the requirements on its path set and assignment are stated in (H4), and the cited DAG results and their limits are stated immediately after the hypothe-ses. In the boundary notation below, aw,D means the node value aw in (I1), the common district-compatible edge value in (I2), and the active or reference value assigned to district D

by the path formula of (I3). In every case we require (5.2) D(HI) ⊆ I (G).

For D ∈ D (HI), define the boundary configuration cI

Ancestrality in (5.1) places every non-source parent of an active district in RI, and the class-specific assignment — the node value in (I1), the complete-source common value in (I2), and the path-formula value in (I3) — fixes every remaining parent, so these are the only two possibilities; Appendix A.4.4 of the Supplementary Material records why this closure is a consequence here rather than a condition attributed to Theorem 1 of [19]. 

Hypotheses of the causal identification assertion. The causal content of Proposition 5.1 — that the configured product identifies a causal response — is asserted under the following hypotheses. (H1) G is the latent projection of a hidden-variable causal DAG and the observed law P

is strictly positive; together with the causal model in (H2), this places P in N+(G) and makes every fixing divisor below positive. (H2) I belongs to one of the classes (I1)–(I3), Y ∩ AI = ∅, and P is generated by the causal model attached to the class: the hidden-variable causal DAG model under which [13, The-orem 48] is stated for (I1); Pearl’s functional model with mutually independent response-function families for (I2) [19, Theorem 1]; the nonparametric structural equation model with independent errors for the direct branch of (I3) [16, Supplement A.2]; the branch of (I3) reduced to (I2) inherits every (I2) hypothesis. (H3) Active intrinsicness (5.2) holds. In (I2), and hence in the reduced branch of (I3), ev-ery district of GV \AI is intrinsic in G as well, a condition that coincides with (5.2) in the system-wide case Y = V \ AI (Appendix A.4.4 of the Supplementary Material). In the direct branch of (I3), no recanting district exists and the total-effect district factors re-quired by the cited path-specific result are identified, which (5.2) supplies through [13, Theorem 48]. (H4) The class-specific compatibility holds: complete-source assignment with one common value aa,D ∈ Xa per source–district pair in (I2); the same within-district coherence and a P -independent value in Xw for every excluded-source tail coordinate in (I3); in every class every boundary value satisfies aw,D ∈ Xw. In the direct branch of (I3), the path set is proper, live and consistent for Y . In the reduced branch of (I3), the path set is proper, 

Y -live and Y -consistent with an edge-consistent assignment in the formal sense of [20], 20 

the equality with the induced intervention of class (I2) is independently justified, and every (I2) hypothesis — its causal model, the complete-source convention, the common assignment, and the intrinsicness of every district of GV \AI — is inherited. The cited results are stated on a DAG: Section 5.3 of [20] defines the edge-consistent path intervention, its Lemma 5.7 gives the equality with the induced edge intervention, and its Corollary 5.2 the identification result, all under the multiple-world model on a DAG; no general observed-ADMG path-to-edge reduction is inferred from those underlying-DAG statements. The causal interpretation of Proposition 5.1 uses the class-specific identification hypothe-ses (H1)–(H4) at the causal law. The statistical extension that follows, from Theorem 5.5 onward, ranges over every law in N+(G) for the fixed graph and intervention configuration — strict positivity remains part of its domain — and requires neither a latent realization nor a causal reading of nearby laws (Remark 5.7). Its operative graphical and configuration conditions are active intrinsicness (5.2), the first clause of (H3), and the fixed, compatible, single-valued boundary selection cI 

> D

with aw,D ∈ Xw, the boundary clause of (H4); together they define the selection map Sel I below. PROPOSITION 5.1 (Configured product and identified response). Under (H1) –(H4) , with

EXAMPLE 5.2 (Why a mixed source boundary is not identified). Consider A → B,

A → B′, and B ↔ B′ with A ∼ Bernoulli( p), 0 < p < 1. Section B.5 of the Supplemen-tary Material specifies two latent-variable models for this graph that induce the same strictly positive observed law. Setting only the arrow A → B to zero while A → B′ retains the nat-ural value of A asks for the joint law of the split responses B(0) and B′(1) — B with the arrow A → B set to 0 and B′ with the arrow A → B′ at the natural value — given the natural value A = 1 , the counterfactual that a kernel indexed by the mixed boundary 

(aA→B , a A→B′ ) = (0 , 1) would have to represent. Write Ki(a) := Law i{B(0) , B ′(a) | A =

a} for the natural-retention response in model i. The two models agree on every observed-kernel slice P( B = b, B ′ = b′ | A = a), but K1(1) and K2(1) are at total-variation distance 

0.64 , while K1(0) = K2(0) ; averaging over the natural draw of A leaves the two split-response laws at distance 0.64 p > 0. Setting both arrows to zero gives the same law in the two models. The split intervention deliberately violates the complete-source condition of (I2): it shows why enlarging the theorem to such mixed boundaries would be invalid. Thus a kernel boundary that asks one source coordinate to be simultaneously natural and intervened within one district need not be a functional of the observed law. 5.2. Active-coordinate selection and normalized reassembly. Fix an intervention of one of the classes (I1)–(I3) and abbreviate R = RI, H = HI, and A = AI. Every intrinsic set S

of H lies in a unique D ∈ D (H). By (5.2), D is reachable in G. Lemma 4.9 of [7], applied within H, first identifies S as intrinsic in dD(H). The CADMGs G[D] and dD(H) have exactly the same directed and bidirected edges among their random vertices D. Fixability, intrinsic subsets, and recursive heads depend only on this random graph. Hence S is intrinsic in G[D]; applying the same lemma in G then identifies S as a global intrinsic set with the same recursive head. Moreover, (5.4) TH(S) = TG (S) ∩ R = TG (S) \ A, 

because any w ∈ TG (S) \ A is either internal to S or has an arrow into S. The internal case gives w ∈ S ⊆ R immediately; in the external case, appending that arrow to an S-to-Y

directed path in GV \A gives w ∈ R. The reverse inclusion follows from H = GR. Define the linear tail-slice map Sel I : UG → UH by (5.5)  Sel I θ

COROLLARY 5.6 (Active-side nested geometry). For every class (I1)–(I3), the config-ured active law ΓI(P ) of Theorem 5.5 is a strictly positive nested Markov law of the active graph H. In particular, at P I = Γ I(P ),

REMARK 5.7 (Statistical, not neighborhood-causal, extension). At the true causal law, 

ΓI(P ) is the configured active law of Proposition 5.1: in classes (I1)–(I2) it is additionally identified as the joint causal response on RI, while in (I3) only its Y margin carries the causal interpretation. Away from that law, equation (5.8) defines the statistical parameter on N+(G).We do not assert that every nearby nested law admits a compatible latent realization, or that the causal identification theorem itself transports to that law. Only the graph, factor indices, boundary values, and the linear map Sel I are fixed throughout the chart. This is the distinction that prevents the latent-margin model from re-entering the operative statistical scope. 5.3. Order-free differentiation and the canonical gradient. 5.4. Independent-draw stochastic and source-standardized targets. The last extension averages the configured active laws over one boundary assignment drawn afresh from a pos-sibly population-dependent law on a fixed finite assignment set, independently of the natural source variables and of the structural disturbances, although the coordinates of the drawn assignment vector may be dependent (Definition 5.10). When the weights depend on the population, differentiating the mixture mean of (5.16) adds the weight derivative αμK of Theo-rem 5.12 to the averaged fixed-component derivative. Every component is a configured active law with the common excluded set, active set and active graph and is differentiated through its own active chart; the mixture law itself receives no chart, since it need not belong to the nested model of the active graph, and identification of the mixture mean is distinguished below from identification of the whole mixture law. Let VI be a fixed nonempty finite set of boundary assignments. For every a ∈ V I, assume that I(a) is a fixed intervention of one of the classes (I1)–(I3) satisfying Proposition 5.1, with the same excluded set A, active set R, and active graph H. REMARK 5.15 (Independent redraw is not natural-value retention). Equation (5.20) draws a fresh joint source vector independently from its observed marginal. It does not set the response to the unit’s naturally realized source vector, which retains the natural source– disturbance coupling and is not represented in general by the first line of (5.16). The latter natural-value construction is outside the present theorem. COROLLARY 5.16 (Complementary blocks for a root singleton source). Let μ be either a fixed exogenous law or the marginal source-standardization rule (5.20) , and suppose A =

{w}, pa G (w) = ∅, and dis G (w) = {w}. For the native source district Kw = {w},

> Kw

= 0 , αμK = 0 (K̸ = Kw).

Thus a fixed or exogenous intervention has no Kw contribution, whereas under (5.20) every 

Kw contribution comes from the weight term, which is nonzero whenever w 7 → ψw is non-constant. For a general admissible P -dependent rule, αμK need not vanish for K̸ = Kw; see Remark 5.17. 

REMARK 5.17 (The source-law chain-rule term). For a general admissible P -dependent law, the correct weight coordinate is the weight line of (5.17); the observed-data formula (5.21) is specific to marginal source standardization. Dropping αμK is a chain-rule error and in general destroys the Riesz identity on the native directions on which the source-law derivative is nonzero. For instance, take binary W, Y, Z in the DAG W → Y with Z

isolated. The admissible rule μθ(1) = P θ(Z = 1) , with μθ(0) = 1 − μθ(1) , gives αμ

If several intervention-induced active channels descend from one native district, their co-ordinate derivative vectors are summed within that district and the same native Gram inverse is applied to every channel. By linearity, summation may precede or follow this operation; treating the channels as separate orthogonal blocks with their own metrics is not valid. 

For the fixed-node, complete-source edge, and path-specific functionals of the classes (I1)– (I3), Theorem 5.5 supplies the identification-to-chart bridge on the entire positive coordinate 26 

domain. Theorem 5.8 then differentiates the active chart and solves the finite-dimensional Riesz problem directly. Thus no ordered change-of-measure argument or separately chosen saturated-model gradient is needed for the abstract EIF. A saturated off-model extension would be noncanonical unless specified; the canonical gradient in (5.15) is intrinsic to the operative nested model. This order-free result does not by itself validate an anchored or sequential observed-data representation based on a single vertex order; Section 7 states what such a construction ad-ditionally requires. Admissible finite independent-draw mixtures of the configured compo-nent laws of those classes are covered by Theorem 5.12, which adds the source-law weight derivative to the averaged fixed-component derivative, and by its source-standardized spe-cialization, Corollary 5.14, within the scope fixed in Remark 5.15. Applied to the direction h of Section 4, which is observationally centered but not a model score, the projection removes a nonzero component h − ΠTP N (G)h, whose squared relative norm ∥h − ΠTP N (G)h∥2

> P, 2

/∥h∥2 

> P, 2

is strictly positive at the law of that section (exact value in Section B.1 of the Supplementary Material). That component is a projection diagnostic, not an efficiency correction: h is a diagnostic direction, not an influence function identified for a specified target, and describing it as one would require first exhibiting a functional whose derivative it represents. 6.1. Coherent chart plug-in and local stability. On fixed finite support the chart infor-mation matrix is positive definite at every interior law (Corollary 3.2), so a regular interior nested-model maximum likelihood estimator is root-n and its plug-in target is efficient by the delta method: efficiency itself needs no targeting in this idealization. The constructions of this section use coherent chart laws as initial estimators, including regularized, cross-fitted, or approximately solved fits that are not interior full-chart likelihood solutions. Proposition 6.12 additionally allows approximations of the Gram and derivative coefficients at a single such law. Fits assembled from mutually incompatible modules, an estimated score basis, or a sep-arately estimated target are not analysed. The coherent one-step estimator and the chart-flow TMLE differ in their exact guarantees; that comparison is made in Section 6.4, and the null-update facts for the two estimators at an interior likelihood fit are recorded in the remarks following Corollaries 6.10 and 6.17. For the remainder of this section, Ψ is either a fixed-response mean of the classes (I1)–(I3) from Theorem 5.8 or an admissible independent-draw mixture mean from Theorem 5.12. The same arguments apply to any functional whose chart representation and canonical-gradient map have the smoothness asserted below. Throughout this section, the graph, vertex state spaces, chart dimension, finite intervention-assignment set, and (when used) number of folds are fixed. We equip UG with the Euclidean norm induced by its fixed coordinate ordering and write it as ∥ · ∥ ; because d is fixed, any other norm on UG gives the same rate conditions. Thus the coherent population one-step correction removes an arbitrary interior error in the source marginal exactly, provided every complementary chart block is correct. 

REMARK 6.6 (One-sided population robustness, not double robustness). Proposition 6.5 is a global identity on the positive source-block slice, not merely a local quadratic bound. There is no reverse robustness statement: correctness of the source marginal alone does not force R2 = 0 , and simultaneous errors in complementary outcome-side coordinates can leave a nonzero drift. The result is therefore one-sided source-block robustness of the coherent population one-step correction, not double or multiple robustness. Nor does the proposition establish robustness of the chart-flow TMLE under a fixed, ar-bitrarily misspecified source marginal. The targeting field in (6.20) generally has nonzero complementary coordinates, so its integral curve need not remain on the source-only slice; its updated-law remainder is R2(θ⋆, θ 0), not R2(η, θ 0). The local chart-TMLE theorem re-mains valid under its stated op(n−1/4) full-chart rate, but no union-model TMLE claim is made. Let P = Pθ0 , and let X1, . . . , X n be i.i.d. from P . Partition the sample, independently of the observations, into a fixed number L of folds Iv , with nv/n → ρv ∈ (0 , 1) ; throughout, condition on the realized fold assignment. Let bθ−v use only observations outside Iv , and compute every object in (6.2) at the single fitted law bP−v = Pbθ−v

. Write Pn,v for the empirical law on Iv . The compact set K in Proposition 6.2 is convex. Thus every segment joining θ0 to an admitted bθ−v ∈ K remains in K, as required by Lemma 6.4; a proposed step that leaves this compact positive-chart neighborhood is not covered by the rate theorem. REMARK 6.9. Corollary 6.8 is an exact finite-sample statement conditional on every complementary chart block being fixed at its true value. It is an oracle comparison that iso-lates error in the source marginal. When the complementary blocks are estimated, their error is governed by Theorem 6.7 and Proposition 6.12 under the rate conditions stated there, rather than by the exact identity. Without convergence of the fitted source blocks the corollary does not by itself provide asymptotic normality, the efficient limit law, or a stable asymptotic vari-ance in Theorem 6.7. The conclusion does not extend automatically to same-sample fitting or to a modular approximation of the canonical gradient. COROLLARY 6.10 (Cross-fitting is optional on fixed finite support). Let bθ be a same-sample estimator with bθ ∈ ΩG almost surely, so that the display below is defined on every sample, and suppose ∥bθ − θ0∥ = op(n−1/4). Because K is a neighborhood of θ0, bθ ∈ K with probability tending to one, and the bounds of Proposition 6.2 are used only there. Then 

bΨos := F (bθ) + Pnϕbθ

obeys (6.13) .

Corollary 6.10 thus contains the efficient plug-in as the case of a null correction; its content lies in the non-likelihood initial fits described at the start of this section. COROLLARY 6.17 (Substitution and range preservation). With the fallback convention in Theorem 6.16, pθ⋆ ∈ N +(G) on every sample, and bΨtmle is the target functional evaluated at that law. If the response transform satisfies a ≤ g ≤ b, then 

a ≤ bΨtmle ≤ b. 

In particular, an indicator-response TMLE lies in [0 , 1] .

REMARK 6.18 (Null update at an interior likelihood fit). If bθ solves the full interior likelihood score equations, then Pnbnat  

> K, bθ

= 0 for every K and hence Pnϕbθ = 0 . Thus zero is exactly the targeting score root. Under the local strictness in Theorem 6.16, the near-zero root selected there is bϵ = 0 , and the TMLE equals the MLE plug-in. Targeting is therefore not needed for efficiency in the regular fixed-dimensional MLE benchmark. Its roles here are to update coherent non-MLE, regularized, or approximately solved initial fits, to preserve the substitution property, and to provide a model-valid template for later regimes in which the MLE benchmark disappears. REMARK 6.19 (Straight-line and numerical implementations). The fixed-direction path 

η + ϵζ (η) is model-valid for sufficiently small ϵ, and its score at zero equals ϕη . Away from zero its score is JPη+ϵζ (η) ζ(η), not generally the canonical gradient at the updated law. Itera-tive targeting must therefore recompute ζ after each update; convergence to an exact EIF root requires its own numerical argument. Likewise, a discretized solution of (6.21) solves the exact empirical equation only up to nu-merical error. To inherit (6.26), a terminal score residual by itself is not sufficient: it does not control numerical error transverse to the exact flow. It is sufficient, for example, to make the terminal coordinate error relative to θ⋆ op(n−1/2). Equivalently, one may require a numerical endpoint eθ to be op(n−1/2)-close to a point ϑbθ(eϵ) on the exact local flow with |eϵ| ≤ δ with probability tending to one, and also require Pnϕeθ = op(n−1/2). Writing Hn(ϵ) := Pnϕϑbθ (ϵ),the sup-norm line of (6.3) gives |Hn(eϵ)| ≤ | Pnϕeθ| + C∥eθ − ϑbθ(eϵ)∥ = op(n−1/2), and the slope bound in (A.10) of the Supplementary Material then gives |eϵ − bϵ| ≤ 4|Hn(eϵ)|/σ 2 =

op(n−1/2), after which bounded flow velocity completes the comparison with θ⋆. The re-striction |eϵ| ≤ δ is needed: the flow exists on (−ϵ0, ϵ 0), whereas the negative-slope bound is established only on the smaller interval [−δ, δ ], so a root outside it is not excluded. Replacing 

Gnat  

> K

or γK by modular approximations similarly targets the modular score rather than ϕθ ;the errors in Proposition 6.12 must then be carried into the targeting equation. REMARK 6.20 (Robustness boundary). Neither the metric projection, the empirical EIF equation, nor the mixture product expansions imply double or multiple robustness: such a claim requires a separately proved drift factorization into products of errors of separately estimable nuisance functionals, which the exact remainder (6.7) is not, and the pure-curvature terms in (6.5) remain. Section 7 proves such a factorization for a different, source-isolated fixed-node sequential construction; it does not strengthen the order-free one-step or TMLE claims of this section. EXAMPLE 6.21 (The universal flow need not preserve the source-only slice). Consider the binary DAG W → X and W → Z, with no edge between X and Z. Source-standardize the intervention on W and take g(X, Z ) = XZ . Write 

π = P θ(W = 1) , rw = P θ(X = 1 | W = w),sw = P θ(Z = 1 | W = w), mw = rwsw.34 

Then 

F (θ) = (1 − π)m0 + πm 1, ϕθ = mW − F (θ) + sW (X − rW ) + rW (Z − sW ).

In these probability coordinates, the targeting field is 

˙π = π(1 − π)( m1 − m0), ˙rw = rw(1 − rw)sw, ˙sw = sw(1 − sw)rw.

Every factor pair has the same sign, so each summand is positive. Thus exact cancellation at the initial source-only error does not imply exact robustness after universal-flow targeting. Under Theorem 6.16’s local full-chart rate the displayed drift is still second order; arbitrary fixed source misspecification is outside that theorem. 6.4. One-step correction and chart targeting: different exact guarantees. The coherent one-step estimator and chart-flow TMLE use the same order-free canonical gradient. Un-der their common local regime, the chart-flow TMLE and the same-sample coherent one-step estimator formed from the same initial law are asymptotically equivalent; both one-step versions have the same efficient first-order expansion. Thus there is no first-order efficiency trade-off between them. Their exact guarantees nevertheless differ, as summarized in Table 1. The efficiency and source-block entries concern different regimes: efficiency requires full-chart convergence to θ0 (Theorems 6.7 and 6.16), whereas the exact cancellation allows an arbitrary interior source-block error only because every complementary block is held at its true value (Proposition 6.5 and the remark following it). Neither full-chart efficiency under fixed source misspecification nor an exact source-robust TMLE follows: the exact chart flow enforces model membership and the inherited response range, but its nonlinear update can leave the source-only slice (Example 6.21). This is a limited contrast between one-sided drift cancellation and substitution, not an efficiency-versus-double- or multiple-robustness theorem. REMARK 6.22 (Comparison with existing doubly robust ADMG estimators). Under pri-mal fixability, Theorem 7 of [3] establishes variational independence of the portions of the observed-data law used by the primal and dual IPW representations, and [3, Theorem 9] establishes double robustness of augmented primal IPW under the associated union model; neither result requires mb-shieldedness. Mb-shieldedness has a different role in their analysis: it makes the equality restrictions DAG-like and supplies the vertexwise tangent decomposi-tion used for the constrained-model efficient projection [3, Theorem 2, Lemma 3, and Theo-rem 12], and it is not the source of their double robustness. beyond mb-shielded ADMGs therefore neither forecloses a doubly robust construction nor confers one on the order-free estimators studied here. The two contributions are complementary but logically distinct. Those authors establish union-model double robustness for their augmented primal IPW estimator in the primal-fixable setting; we establish an order-free canonical gradient and model-valid efficient in-ference in the strictly positive finite-state nested model for the intervention classes (I1)–(I3) on arbitrary ADMGs. Neither result implies the other, and we prove no double- or multiple-robustness guarantee for that full class: the transitionwise union result of Section 7 holds only under Assumption 7.2, and Proposition 6.5 is the exact one-sided source-block result available here, with the scope stated in the remark following it, not an analogue of the pri-mal/dual union model. Such robustness is not needed for first-order efficiency in the likeli-hood benchmark described at the start of this section, but it remains meaningful for regular-ized or approximately solved fits, for which it requires a drift factorization and rate conditions appropriate to those fits. The 30-versus-23 dimension gap in Section 4 rules out arbitrary normalized district-kernel tilting, not every alternative nuisance decomposition or factorized drift; recursive-head-compatible factorized drifts over the full non-mb-shielded class are not characterized here. 

7. A restricted sequential union-model alternative. The order-free estimator in Sec-tion 6 is built from the canonical gradient and applies to every intervention covered there. This section constructs a second estimator for a narrower fixed-node subclass. Its purpose is different: it preserves a transitionwise factorization of the population drift and is consequently consistent on a union of nuisance correctness regimes. At the all-correct intersection its influ-ence function is generally not canonical. Thus the comparison below is between two specific 36 

constructions—an efficient recursive-head estimator and a restricted union-model sequential estimator; what that comparison does and does not establish is stated after Proposition 7.8. The restrictions stated below—source isolation, an acyclic literal block order with source precedence, and source-tail coverage—are the conditions this ordinary-propensity construc-tion uses; they are not claimed to be necessary for every possible robust estimator. The next subsection shows by example that three natural shortcuts fail, and the examples motivate the conditions that follow; the full-history transport identity is then proved and every estimation claim is derived from it. 7.1. Three shortcuts that fail. The first shortcut deletes earlier source values from the histories of later propensities. The product of the resulting inverse propensities need not have mean one, so it need not define a change of probability. At the exact binary law for A1 ≺ A2,the inverse-probability product anchored at (1 , 1) , using the two marginal denominators, has expectation 1.6, whereas keeping A1 = 1 in the second denominator replaces 0.5 by 0.8 and makes the expectation one. The second shortcut allows an intervention source inside a transition block by restricting the order only among active vertices. The graph is L ↔ Y with L → A → Y under do( A =1) . The active graph has the single district {L, Y }, so the literal block-order condition is vacuous after restricting to the active vertices. Nevertheless every observational topological order has L ≺ A ≺ Y : the source lies inside the active block. A candidate weight may then depend on L, part of the current transition, and the true district residual need not be centered against that weight. The third shortcut substitutes conditioning for fixing at a source that is bidirected-confounded with its district, even when the source precedes the block. In the graph A ↔ Y ,fixing A under a node intervention gives the kernel qY (y) = p(y), whereas conditioning on 

A gives p(y | a). At a strictly positive binary law the two probabilities of Y = 1 are 0.5 and 

0.8, respectively, so the ordinary weight 1(A = 1) /P( A = 1) transports to the wrong law. Placing the source before the block does not exclude this example: what removes A from a bidirected district is the fixing divisor p(a | y), not a propensity measurable with respect to the past. Section B.10 of the Supplementary Material states the three exact laws and carries the examples in full: the anchored expectation 1.6 rather than one, the drift 0.05625 rather than zero, and the fixed-against-conditioned probabilities 0.5 and 0.8.The examples motivate the three conditions imposed below: propensities conditioned on the full strict past, precedence of every source relevant to a transition over that transition, and, for the present theorem, intervention sources that are singleton observational districts. The last condition is sufficient rather than necessary; removing it requires a fixing-based or primal–dual weighting theory and is outside the present paper. 7.2. The sequential scope conditions. Fix a system-wide fixed-node intervention I from (I1), put A = AI, V ∗ = V \ A, and let H = GV ∗ . Write β ∈ XA for the intervention’s assigned value vector, with coordinates βa, a ∈ A.

The minimal choice is AD = pa G (D) \ V ∗, the set of excluded parents of D: exactly the sources occupying an external tail slot of the native D-factor. Larger source sets are permit-ted. The node intervention assigns the value sub-vector βD := ( βa)a∈AD to the sources in

Under source isolation no bidirected edge meets A, so the active districts are the districts of G other than the singletons {a}, and BI is the district partition of G itself. Insert every observational directed arrow between distinct blocks and, as a formal precedence constraint, insert {a} → D whenever a ∈ AD . With the minimal source sets every precedence arrow duplicates an observational arrow, so the block graph is the district quotient of G (Section 3) and its acyclicity is acyclicity of that quotient; each additional source placed in some AD adds one precedence constraint, which is the reason for the general formulation. The precedence arrows constrain an order; they are not causal edges. PROPOSITION 7.1 (MR-compatible block-order screen). The directed block graph in 

(7.3) is acyclic if and only if the observational directed graph has a topological order ≺ in which every block in BI is a literal interval and every a ∈ AD precedes every vertex of D.

Uω , which concern population candidates; the estimation conditions of Theorem 7.6, which concern the fitted nuisances; and a fitting procedure producing candidates that lie in a regime, which this paper does not specify. The fixtures of Section B.7 of the Supplementary Material verify the algebra of the regimes at an exact law by offsetting the true nuisances; they do not demonstrate an implemented misspecification-robust estimator. 40

For m = 1 the terminal function is the known response, ¯Q1 = g(XY ), so the union state-ment in (i) has two regimes: correctness of the weight ( ¯H1 = H01 ), which is consistency of inverse-probability weighting, and correctness of the residual ¯R1 = g(XY )− ¯Q0, which is ex-actly the equality ¯Q0 = ψ0 of the scalar initial value with the target. No covariate-dependent fitted outcome regression enters at m = 1 ; the only fitted outcome quantity is that scalar, and its population correctness is a statement about the candidate, distinct from the estima-tion and rate conditions of the theorem. The exact two-transition example in (B.18)–(B.20) of Section B.7 of the Supplementary Material has both sides of the drift identity equal, and nonzero, under a state-dependent joint misspecification, with the drift carried entirely by the second transition; under the same law the telescoping-constant construction verifies all four correctness regimes, and the three controls in (B.21) separately activate the first drift sum-mand, the second, and both. That section states every offset and weight scale explicitly, so that each calculation is reproducible from the paper. Part (ii) is intentionally an intersection result. Under fixed misspecification of the unneeded side of a union component, consistency may remain true, but a root-n limit requires separate rates and its influence function generally depends on the nuisance probability limits. Union-model consistency must not be reported as automatically efficient or as having the all-correct influence function (7.16). 7.4. Projection and the efficiency comparison.

satisfies all 24 recursive-head coordinate Riesz equations but lies outside the nested-model tangent space. The canonical gradient is its strict metric projection. 

In this one-transition example ϕseq is a pure inverse-probability-weighted residual: since 

B0 = ∅, it contains no covariate-dependent outcome regression, although the estimator re-mains augmented through bQ0 and keeps the two regimes of Theorem 7.6(i), which for 

m = 1 are weight correctness and the scalar condition ¯Q0 = ψ0 noted after that theorem. Its canonical projection uses the recursive-head restrictions and removes the orthogonal residual recorded in Proposition 7.8; by (7.19), equal-level asymptotic Wald intervals are wider for the sequential estimator by the factor (P0ϕ2seq /P 0ϕ2eff )1/2 = 1 .3116 . . . , about 31% (Section 8). Two sources of gain are distinct. Here the nested and ordinary Markov models coincide (Sec-tion 4), so the gain comes from ordinary conditional independences in a graph that is not mb-shielded; in the one-edge variant of Remark 7.10, the additional Verma restriction alone reduces the efficiency bound at the law P + by approximately 22 .45% , a reduction of variance rather than of interval width (width factor approximately 1.1356 ). The proposition does not say that every sequential influence function is inefficient, that no efficient estimator can be robust on a special submodel, or that efficiency and robustness can never coexist: the comparison is between two specific constructions, and no impossibility theorem is asserted. When the observed-data model is nonparametric the influence function is unique, so an estimator that is regular with respect to the full nonparametric observed-data model and asymptotically linear at the all-correct law necessarily has the canonical gradi-ent, and no projection gap can arise; regularity within one nuisance regime does not suffice for this conclusion. On the graph of Section 4 the operative model N+(G) is a strict sub-model of the observed-data law space, influence functions are correspondingly nonunique, and Theorem 7.7 gives only the inequality P0ϕ2seq ≥ P0ϕ2eff together with its equality condi-tion; strictness is established at the law of Proposition 7.8 and is not asserted for every graph in the scope of this paper. The two-transition calculation in Section B.7 of the Supplementary Material separately checks all four union regimes and a second strict projection gap at a law generated through a latent SEM rather than from recursive-head coordinates. 42

REMARK 7.9 (Reading the count 2m). Counts reported elsewhere index different ob-jects and do not order robustness. For a generalized mediation functional with Kmed causally ordered mediators, [27] obtains estimators that are Kmed + 2 robust in the sense that consis-tency follows if any one of Kmed + 2 specified sets of nuisance functions is correctly specified and consistently estimated, and cautions (Section 1 there, writing K for its mediator count) that this convention “does not imply that a ‘ K + 2 -robust’ estimator is necessarily more ro-bust than, for example, a ‘ K + 1 -robust’ estimator”, since the estimators may correspond to different estimands that require modeling different parts of the likelihood. That result, like Theorem 7.6, concerns a single component functional rather than a contrast. We make no comparison of robustness strength between the two constructions and no impossibility claim for either; the count that the present representation reports for a mediation graph is recorded in Section B.7 of the Supplementary Material. REMARK 7.10 (A one-edge Verma variant). The nested model of the graph in Proposi-tion 7.8 coincides with its ordinary Markov model (Section 4 and Appendix B of the Sup-plementary Material), so the gap in Proposition 7.8 is produced by ordinary conditional in-dependences. Adding the arrow W → B gives a graph G+ with the same districts and the same root singleton source, in which the reachable {B, Y } kernel is no longer constrained to be invariant in w, while its Y -margin, obtained by summing over B, is constrained to be invariant: P 

> b

p(b | w)p(y | b, z, w ) does not depend on w. This is a Verma restriction; the ordinary Markov model of G+ is {W ⊥⊥ A}, and the chart dimensions are 28 < 30 < 31 . At the strictly positive law P + of (B.22) in Appendix B, do( W = 0) satisfies Assumption 7.2 with m = 1 , ψ0 = 0 .542 and P +ϕ2seq = 0 .496472 . At this law, imposing the ordinary Markov restriction leaves the efficiency bound unchanged, whereas the additional Verma restriction reduces it by approximately 22 .45% , from 0.496472 to approximately 0.38500 (exact values in (B.23)); the corresponding interval widths differ by the factor approximately 1.1356 .ORDER-FREE INFERENCE IN NESTED MARKOV MODELS 43 

8. Finite-sample illustration of the variance comparison. We illustrate the strict com-parison in Proposition 7.8 at its rational law. The target is ψ0 = E P0 {Y | do( W = 0) } =0.54725 ; the samples are i.i.d. draws of size n = 1000 , 2500 , and 5000 , with 500 Monte Carlo replicates at each size and fixed seed 20260722 , and every estimator is evaluated on every replicate. Three estimators are compared: the same-sample one-step estimator F (eθ) + Pnϕeθ

of Corollary 6.10, the practical iterated chart-targeted estimator of Remark 6.19, and the se-quential estimator in its same-sample pooled-ratio form, whose influence function at P0 is the all-correct sequential influence function ϕseq of Proposition 7.8; Section B.9 of the Sup-plementary Material records the pooled-ratio identity and its distinction from the cross-fitted estimator (7.13), the standard errors, the initialization, targeting and fallback settings, and the complete results. The influence-function variance constants are exact rationals, estab-lished in the proof of Proposition 7.8 in Section A.4.6 of the Supplementary Material. To five decimals, and so that v/n is the leading asymptotic variance of the corresponding estimator, 

veff ≈ 0.28803 and vseq ≈ 0.49553 , with (vseq /v eff )1/2 = 1 .311647 . . . . Thus the exact limit theory predicts equal-level asymptotic Wald intervals about 31% wider for the sequential estimator. The observed sequential interval widths (Table 5 of the Supplementary Material) exceed those of the one-step and targeted estimators by approximately 31 .2% to 31 .4% across the three sample sizes, computed from the unrounded replicate summaries recorded in the sup-plement, in agreement with the exact ratio above; the coverage values are descriptive rather than acceptance criteria. This experiment illustrates the efficiency gap under correct specification; it does not test nuisance misspecification or establish the sequential union model, whose two-transition bookkeeping and four correctness regimes are checked exactly in Section B.7 of the Sup-plementary Material. Code, replicate-level results, and all diagnostics are included in the computational supplement. 

9. Discussion. 

What the theory establishes. Three notions concerning a direction f ∈ L2(P ) are kept apart throughout: observational centering, membership in the tangent space TP N (G), and orthogonal projection onto it. The first does not imply the second: the observationally cen-tered direction of Example 4.1, in a graph that is not mb-shielded, is not a model score, and its projection residual is a diagnostic, not an efficiency correction. Three conclusions follow. The forward recursive-head chart settles which directions are model scores (Theo-rem 3.1), and because native-district score spaces are orthogonal at every positive law (Theo-rem 3.8) the efficient projection is computed district by district, with no district order and no acyclicity condition on the district quotient. For the interventions of the classes (I1)–(I3) the canonical gradient is a district-by-district Riesz solution (Theorems 5.5 and 5.8), extended to independent-draw mixtures by a product rule; the coherent one-step and chart-flow tar-geted estimators are first-order efficient under the local conditions of Theorems 6.7 and 6.16, and neither carries a union-model guarantee for the full class (I1)–(I3) (Table 1). The dou-ble robustness of [3] comes from the primal–dual structure under primal fixability, not from mb-shieldedness. The restricted sequential construction is transitionwise multiply robust only under Assumption 7.2 (Theorem 7.6)—intervention sources that are singleton observational districts, a literal block order in which each relevant source precedes its district, and source-tail coverage, whose separate necessity is not asserted—and its all-correct influence function has variance at least the efficiency bound, strictly larger at the law of Proposition 7.8 (Theo-rem 7.7). No general trade-off between efficiency and robustness is asserted (Remark 7.9). 44 

Limits and extensions. The finite-state restriction is what makes the chart a finite-dimensional smooth manifold, the paths explicit, and the projection a matrix computation; it is also what makes the full-chart likelihood benchmark available. Boundary laws, vanishing fixing divisors, and nonregular targets lie outside the interior theory. Three questions are left open. First, extending this route beyond fixed finite support would need a smooth parameter-ization with an explicit tangent space, a computable projection onto it, and a replacement for strict positivity; whether other routes need the same ingredients is not asserted, and the bench-mark of Section 6, under which a regular interior maximum likelihood plug-in is efficient without targeting, would no longer be available. Second, the efficient projection separates across native districts, but within a district the full Gram matrix across its intrinsic blocks is in general required (Section 3), so scalable computation of the projection at larger finite state spaces is open; the chart dimension alone is not an established complexity bound. Third, the order-free estimators lack a constructive nuisance decomposition supporting robustness: one would need a factorized drift compatible with recursive-head coordinates, which Section 6 does not characterize and which Section 7 obtains only under Assumption 7.2. The intervention classes are a matter of identification scope rather than of support. Natural-value source retention, partially mixed edge boundaries (Example 5.2), and general observed-ADMG path-to-edge reductions are outside the theorems already on fixed finite support, and are not unfinished cases of them. Every later appeal to the empty case cites this convention. The independence clause in (A.1) is part of the definition; it is not implicit in the phrase random-ancestral margin. To use one quantifier throughout the induction, define the class of retained random sets 

AR(H) := {R ∩ A : A ⊆ R ∪ W is ancestral in H} .

For AR ∈ AR(H), put WAR := pa H(AR) \ AR. The independence clause in (RF2), together with the usual deletion of childless fixed vertices and suppression of their irrelevant argu-ments, makes the margin in (A.1) a canonical kernel on H[AR] with fixed set WAR . Below, a retained ancestral margin always means this kernel. The passage from the arguments xW ∩A

to xWAR is itself an instance of (RF2): the set A′ := AR ∪ WAR is ancestral, because the random parents of AR lie in A ∩ R = AR and fixed vertices have no parents, and (RF2) ap-plied to A′ makes the margin independent of xW \WAR whenever AR̸ = R; when AR = R,reducedness gives W = WR and nothing is removed.

This identity is purely algebraic and permits signed factors. Lemma 4.14 of that paper supplies the partition split; Definition 4.1 and Lemma 4.6 ensure that every intrinsic-set block belongs to exactly one district. The following positivity consequence must not be inferred from normalization alone. 50 

LEMMA A.1 (Signed-factor positivity). Let H = ( R, W, E H) be a finite-state CADMG and let η ∈ UH. If ΦH(η) is strictly positive, then every factor on the right of (A.2) is strictly positive. Consequently, Corollary 2.4 makes each such factor a probability kernel. 

PROOF . Induct simultaneously over all finite-state CADMGs and coordinate vectors, or-dered by |R|. The claim is vacuous for R = ∅ and immediate for one random vertex. For the induction step, choose a sterile random vertex a, which exists because the directed random subgraph is acyclic, and let D⋆ be its district. By Lemma 2.3,

> xa
