is strictly positive. The induction hypothesis makes every district factor of this reduced for-ward array strictly positive. For an original district D̸ = D⋆, deletion of a changes neither 

H[D] nor its factor: sterility precludes a from being a parent-tail argument, and a is not bidirected-connected to D. Hence all original factors except possibly the D⋆ factor are strictly positive. Algebraic district splitting now gives that last factor as the positive quotient 

ΦH[D⋆](η|D⋆ ) = ΦH(η)

This proves the lemma without ordering the districts; in particular, it does not assume that the district quotient is acyclic. 

Parameterized kernel implies recursive factorization. Suppose that qR = Φ H(η) > 0 is a probability kernel. We prove by induction on |R| that it satisfies (RF1) and (RF2). The one-vertex case is the base case of Definition 3.3. For (RF1), use (A.2). The signed-factor positivity lemma and Corollary 2.4 show that the displayed factors are positive probability kernels. If there is more than one district, every district has fewer than |R| random vertices, so the induction hypothesis makes each factor recursively factorize according to its district CADMG. If there is one district, the required factorization is the tautology qR = qR.For (RF2), fix an ancestral A ⊆ R ∪ W with R \ A̸ = ∅ and put AR := R ∩ A. No random vertex outside AR can have a directed path into AR. Repeatedly choose, from the currently remaining part of R \ AR, a vertex maximal in a topological ordering. Ancestrality excludes a child in AR, so this vertex is sterile in the current CADMG. Iterating Lemma 2.3 yields (A.3) X

> xR\AR

ΦH(η)( xR | xW ) = Φ H[AR](η|AR )( xAR | xWAR ).

The right side is independent of every discarded fixed argument; because A is ancestral, 

WAR ⊆ W ∩ A. It is also strictly positive, as a margin of qR, and has fewer random vertices. The induction hypothesis therefore gives recursive factorization according to H[AR]. This proves (RF2), including its fixed-argument independence clause. 

Recursively factorizing kernel implies parameterization. Now suppose that qR > 0 re-cursively factorizes according to H. We use the strengthened induction in the proof of The-orem 5.4 of [7]. For every CADMG with m random vertices and every positive recursively factorizing kernel qR, we prove the following three claims simultaneously: 

(E m) qR = Φ H(η(R)) for a vector η(R) ∈ UH produced by the construction ;(U m) Φ H(η) = qR, η ∈ UH =⇒ η = η(R);(C m) η(AR) = η(R)|AR for every proper AR ∈ AR(H).ORDER-FREE INFERENCE IN NESTED MARKOV MODELS 51 

Here η(AR) is the vector recovered at the lower induction level from the canonical (RF2) margin on H[AR]; for AR = ∅ it is the empty vector. At level m, assume all three claims at smaller levels, prove (E m) and then (C m), and finally prove (U m). Thus existence never invokes uniqueness at the same level. Appendix C of the cited paper announces the finite-state adaptation; the details below make the noncorner-state repetition and the corner-state reconstruction explicit. 

Proof of (E m). Write η = η(R) for the vector constructed at the current level. For m = 0 the empty vector gives the unique empty kernel. For m = 1 , write R = {v} and T = pa H(v). For every a ∈ eXv , set 

η{v}(a | xT ) := qv(a | xT ).

occurs in at least one of these margins. Indeed, let H have intrinsic set C. in that margin. If a head occurs in several margins, their finite intersection remains in AR(H);repeated application of Corollary 4.10 of [7] retains that head in the intersection. Marginaliz-ing either larger margin to this intersection gives the same margin of qR. By the lower-level consistency assertion (C m′ ), m′ < m , applied in each margin H[R \ { h}] containing the head, the vector recovered there restricts on the common intersection to the vector recovered from that intersection, which (U m′′ ) at the level m′′ < m ′ of the intersection makes unique; all copies of the shared block therefore coincide. Thus all blocks except the top block indexed by the intrinsic set R have been recovered consistently. The secondary induction therefore proves equality at every cell. This is the general-state version of the last induction in the proof of Theorem 5.4; the sum over all zh ∈ eXh is the step suppressed by the binary notation. 

Proof of (C m). Let AR ∈ AR(H) be proper. Iterating Lemma 2.3 in reverse topological order gives 

qAR|WAR = Φ H[AR](η(R)|AR ),

with irrelevant fixed arguments suppressed. The induction uniqueness hypothesis for this smaller kernel makes η(R)|AR its recovered vector η(AR). This proves (C m).Combining the two directions proves clause (i) of Proposition 2.5 on the strictly positive domain used in the manuscript. The corresponding boundary-kernel equivalence is given in [7, Theorem 5.4 and Appendix C]. At the boundary, however, coordinates on a zero tail-margin stratum are versions and may be changed without altering qR; an algebraic district factor formed from an arbitrary such version need not itself be nonnegative on that stratum. This is why neither the proposition nor the argument above attributes factor nonnegativity to algebraic normalization. A.3.2. Uniqueness and coordinate identification. Proof of (U m). Let η(R) be the vector constructed in (E m), and suppose that ΦH(η) = qR for another η ∈ UH. At a strictly positive recursively factorizing kernel, Proposition 3.6, Lemma 3.8 and Corollary 3.9 of [7] show that every district factor and every kernel obtained by the recursive reductions is uniquely defined and strictly positive. Apply the preceding induction to qR = Φ H(η) > 0. In the multiple-district case, the signed-factor positivity lemma and the forward induction make the algebraic factors from η positive recursively factorizing kernels. Proposition 3.6 and Corollary 3.9 make them the unique district factors, and the induction hypothesis fixes their disjoint co-ordinate blocks. In the one-district case, Lemma 2.3 makes every proper retained ancestral margin from η equal the corresponding margin of qR. The induction hypothesis therefore fixes every non-top block; the explicit all-noncorner identity following (A.5) then forces the top block to equal the conditional ratio in (A.4). ORDER-FREE INFERENCE IN NESTED MARKOV MODELS 53 

Thus the induction determines each non-top block from a common ancestral margin and each top block from its conditional ratio. Equivalently, for every intrinsic C, with H = H(C)

and T = T (C), it gives

rC (zH , x C\H | xT \C ) ,

η = η(R), proving (U m) and completing the simultaneous induction. This proves clause (ii) and the freestanding-CADMG part of clause (iii). A.3.3. Compatibility with RRS fixing. This subsubsection proves clauses (ii)–(iii) of Proposition 2.1 in the full form of Proposition A.4 below; clause (i) is by citation. The proofs below cite Corollary 2.4, Proposition 2.5(i) and Lemma 3.4, whose own proofs use only the chart side; they do not cite the cross-framework clause of Proposition 2.5(iii), which is downstream of Proposition 2.1. Two source conventions are used by the intrinsic kernel (2.1) of Section 2. The standing convention immediately following Definition 2.1 of [7], used in the proof of its Lemma 2.9, together with that paper’s Remark 3.2, is what licenses the deletion of ZC and the argument suppression that red C performs in (2.1). Lemma 2.9 of [7] concerns reachability under that paper’s own reduction operations and is not used here to justify Richardson-style fixing reachability. 

Intrinsic sets as terminal random sets. Every intrinsic C ∈ I (H) is reachable as the entire random set of a terminal CADMG whose single district is C: by [13, Definition 33], C is a district in some reachable graph; [13, Proposition 18] supplies a fixable vertex in every other nonempty district; and [13, Proposition 24] shows that fixing such a vertex neither merges nor splits the districts it does not meet, so fixing those vertices in succession leaves C a district, and concatenating the sequences reaches random set C. Section 2 uses this fact to define the intrinsic kernel, and the proof of Lemma A.2 uses it below. 

Graph side of the intrinsic kernel (2.1) . For C ∈ I (G) put eGC := ϕV \C (G). Direct inspec-tion of the graph-fixing operation shows that eGC has random set C, fixed set V \ C, and precisely the original edges whose arrowheads lie in C; deleting the childless fixed vertices 

ZC := ( V \ C) \ pa G (C) leaves exactly G[C]: its fixed set is pa G (C) \ C, its bidirected edges are internal to C, and its directed edges have heads in C. For every district D ∈ D (G) the district CADMG and the reduced CADMG agree, dD(G) = G[D]: at C = D, Definitions 2.5 and 2.8 of [7] specify the same random set D, the same fixed set pa G (D) \ D, the same bidirected edges (both endpoints in D) and the same directed edges (arrowhead in D). LEMMA A.2 (Two notions of intrinsic set coincide). Let H = ( R, W, E H) be a reduced CADMG; raw fixing graphs are compared after deleting their childless, hence isolated, fixed vertices, which changes neither the subgraph induced on the random vertices nor any district. A set C ⊆ R is intrinsic in the sense of [13, Definition 33] if and only if it is intrinsic in the sense of [7, Definition 4.1]; in that case IH(C) = C, and in both senses its recursive head is the sterile subset of C and its tail is pa H(C).

PROOF . Both statements concern graphs only; no kernel is involved. ( ⇐) A recursively intrinsic C is a district of a graph obtained from H by finitely many random-ancestral and district reductions. A random-ancestral reduction to A is realized by fixing the vertices of 

R \ A one at a time in reverse topological order: each is childless in the current graph, hence 54 

fixable. A district reduction to D is realized by fixing, one at a time, a vertex that is maximal, within a district other than D, in a topological order of the current directed graph: such a vertex has no descendant in its own district, hence is fixable, and by [13, Proposition 24] D

remains a district. Fixing deletes only edges with an arrowhead at the fixed vertex, so after every step the subgraph induced on the random vertices is the vertex-induced subgraph of 

H on that set, exactly as after the corresponding recursive reduction; the districts of the two constructions therefore agree at every stage, and the concatenated sequence is valid with C adistrict of its terminal graph. ( ⇒) Let C be a district of ϕω(H) for a valid ω, extended so that 

C is the entire random set, as shown above. Since fixing deletes no edge between random vertices, C is bidirected-connected in H, and it remains so in every vertex-induced subgraph of H that retains C; consequently each district reduction in the construction of the intrinsic closure retains the single district containing C. Put S := IH(C) ⊇ C [7, Definition 4.3]. Stability of S under both reductions then gives that S is bidirected-connected in HS and that every s ∈ S has a directed path to C inside HS . If S̸ = C, let v be the first vertex of S \ C

fixed by ω. When v is fixed every vertex of S is still random, so the bidirected edges inside 

S and a directed path from v to some c ∈ C inside HS are present; hence c ∈ de( v) ∩ dis( v)

with c̸ = v, contradicting fixability. Thus S = C; since S is produced by recursive reductions and is bidirected-connected, C is recursively intrinsic. Heads and tails are determined by C

and the edges of H.Throughout, Pc(G) is the Markov class of [13, Theorem 16], which states Pcf (G) = 

Pcl (G, ≺) = Pcm(G) = Pca(G); through the Tian factorization characterization Pcf , member-ship says that for every ancestral A the margin factorizes as Q 

> D∈D (GA)

qD(xD | xpa( D)\D).The induction below carries recursive factorization, not Pc: the latter is not universally closed under arbitrary fixing of arbitrary Markov kernels — if it were, every ordinarily Markov ker-nel would be nested Markov, which is exactly what Verma constraints deny.

recursively factorize according to H[D]; we claim that Q

Otherwise, by Proposition 2.5(i) at each H[D], rD = Φ H[D](η(D)). By the no-sharing clause of Lemma 3.4, the tagged coordinate blocks of the H[D] partition those of H, so the η(D) assemble to a single η ∈ UH with η|D = η(D), and that lemma gives ΦH(η) = Q

Proposition 2.5(i) now applies and gives recursive factorization. Proposition 2.5 and the chart are invoked directly on H, which is a reduced CADMG; that is the hypothesis under which both are available. LEMMA A.3 (Recursive factorization implies Markov membership). Let H be a reduced finite-state CADMG with random set R and fixed set W , and let qR > 0 recursively factorize according to H [7, Definition 3.3]. Then qR ∈ P c(H), the Markov class of [13, Theorem 16]. If eH adjoins childless fixed vertices to H and eq is the constant extension of q ∈ P c(H), then 

eq ∈ P c( eH). The restriction to reduced CADMGs is necessary. 

PROOF . Markov membership. Let H be reduced and let qR > 0 recursively factorize according to H. By Theorem 16 it is enough to exhibit the Pcf factorization at every ancestral 

A. First consider R = ∅, when the first two cases below coincide because R ∩ A = ∅ = R.ORDER-FREE INFERENCE IN NESTED MARKOV MODELS 55 

Reducedness then forces W = ∅, the kernel is q∅ = 1 , and the Tian product is empty. Now assume R̸ = ∅ and partition on R ∩ A. If R ∩ A = ∅, the margin is the unit empty kernel and the factorization is the empty product. If R ∩ A = R, ancestrality and reducedness give 

W ⊆ A, hence A = R ∪ W ; the factorization is (RF1) if |R| ≥ 2, and if |R| = 1 , say R = {v},reducedness gives W = pa H(v) \ { v}, so qv(· | xW ) already carries exactly the signature of the sole Tian factor and there is nothing to prove. If ∅ ⊊ R ∩ A ⊊ R, invoke (RF2) first: it supplies the correctly indexed recursively factorizing kernel on H[R ∩ A], including its independence of the discarded fixed arguments — taking the margin as the sole Tian factor would not by itself establish that independence, which for proper A is (RF2)’s own clause. If 

R ∩ A is a singleton, that kernel is its sole Tian factor; otherwise its (RF1) clause gives the district factorization over D(H[R∩A]) = D(HA) — both graphs retain exactly the bidirected edges inside R ∩ A, so their components agree — with probability-kernel factors conditioned on pa( D) \ D ⊆ A by ancestrality. This proves the first assertion of the lemma. The restriction to reduced CADMGs is necessary. At the generality of arbitrary finite-state CADMGs, take R = {v}, an isolated fixed vertex z, and any q(xv | xz ) > 0 genuinely depending on xz : if the recursive clauses of Definition 3.3 are extended verbatim to this unreduced CADMG, the singleton case imposes no condition on the extra argument xz , so the extended definition accepts q, while Pcf at A = R ∪ W forces q = q{v}(xv), since z / ∈

pa( v). So that kernel would recursively factorize under the verbatim extension and is not Markov; the standing convention of [7] that every displayed fixed vertex has a child — the reduction convention adopted here — is exactly what excludes the example, and the raw fixing graphs that temporarily violate the convention are handled by reduction, through the constant-extension assertion of Lemma A.3. 

Constant extension. Let eH adjoin a set Z of childless fixed vertices to H and let eq(xR |

xW ∪Z ) := q(xR | xW ) with q ∈ P c(H). By Theorem 16 it suffices to verify Pcf ( eH). Each z ∈

Z is fixed, so no edge has an arrowhead at it, and childless, so it has no outgoing edge either: z

is isolated, hence has no descendants other than itself, is an ancestor of no random vertex, and is a parent of no vertex. Consequently, for every ancestral A of eH, the set A \ Z is ancestral in H, and eHA has the same random vertices, the same districts and the same pa( D) \ D

as HA\Z . The factorization supplied by q ∈ P cf (H) is therefore verbatim a factorization of 

eq(xR∩A | xW ∪Z ), which is the same function; Theorem 16 returns eq ∈ P c( eH). The transfer is routed through Pcf deliberately: the augmented-graph construction behind Pca treats fixed vertices specially, so no all-four-properties shortcut is invoked. PROPOSITION A.4 (Compatibility with RRS fixing, full form). Let G, p and P be as in Proposition 2.1, suppose the equivalent conditions of its clause (i) hold, and let ω be a valid fixing sequence with reachable random set R, the letter w being reserved for a single fixable vertex. Fixing along ω reaches the raw pair eGR := ϕω(G), eqR := ϕω(p; G), and reduction produces the reduced pair (HR, q R). Then: (a) eqR > 0 and is constant in the arguments in-dexed by the childless fixed vertices of eGR, so red applies; (b) qR := red R(eqR) is a strictly positive kernel that recursively factorizes on the reduced CADMG HR; hence qR ∈ P c(HR)

and eqR ∈ P c( eGR) by Lemma A.3; (c) qR depends on ω only through R; and for every reach-able reduced pair (H0, q R0 ) reached by a valid sequence ω0, every C ∈ I (H0), and every admissible Evans–Richardson recursive derivation from H0 to C, choosing any valid RRS fixing sequence σ implementing that derivation, 

rC = red C ϕσ(qR0 ; H0) = red C ϕω0◦σ(p; G) = red C ϕV \C (p; G) = qC ,

the middle equality by the raw lift and the commutation of fixing with constant extension fol-lowed by reduction, the third by Theorem 31 of [13] applied in G, where nested membership 56 

has been established; order-invariance removes the dependence on the chosen σ, so the con-clusion is derivation-level. Here rC is the recursively derived kernel of Proposition 2.5 (iii) 

and qC the intrinsic kernel of (2.1) .

Since (a)–(b) hold for every valid sequence, p satisfies Definition 27 of [13], and Theo-rem 31 of that source then applies; together with (a)–(b) this is clause (ii) of Proposition 2.1, and (c) contains its clause (iii). PROOF . The induction: clauses (a)–(b), that is, clause (ii) of Proposition 2.1. Along a valid ω = ⟨w1, . . . , w k⟩, put, for 0 ≤ j ≤ k,

eHj := ϕ⟨w1,...,w j ⟩(G), eqj := ϕ⟨w1,...,w j ⟩(p; G),

(Hj , q j ) := red( eHj , eqj ), Rj := V \ { w1, . . . , w j }.

Two conditioning domains must be kept apart: the raw kernel eqj conditions on xfWj

with 

fWj := V \ Rj , while the reduced kernel qj conditions on xWj with Wj := pa eHj (Rj ) \ Rj . It is (I1) below that identifies eqj with the constant extension of qj from the reduced to the raw domain; displayed conditionings are on xWj only after that identification. 

The invariant. At every j:(I1) eqj > 0 and is constant in the arguments indexed by the childless fixed vertices of eHj , so 

red is well defined; (I2) qj > 0 recursively factorizes according to Hj ;(I3) eqj ∈ P c( eHj ), so Proposition 25 of [13] applies to the raw pair; (I4) deletion is inert for the next step, in three clauses: (I4a) the district collections of eHj

and Hj are equal; (I4b) for every vertex of the common random set Rj , its descendant set, fixability status and Markov blanket computed in the two graphs agree; (I4c) for every 

v ∈ F( eHj ) = F(Hj ) — the equality being (I4b) — after the (I1) identification of eqj with the constant extension of qj , the fixing divisors eqj (xv | xmb eHj (v)) and qj (xv | xmb Hj (v))

agree as functions on the identified domain, one v at a time. The Rj scoping in (I4b) is needed — the two graphs have different vertex sets, so an unre-stricted clause is not well formed for the deleted vertices — and (I4c) is not a graph statement at all: graph isolation gives graphical equalities, never equality of divisor functions, which is why it uses (I1). Why the graphical clauses hold: in a CADMG no edge has an arrowhead at a fixed vertex and bidirected edges have both endpoints random, so a fixed vertex’s only possible edges are outgoing, and a childless fixed vertex is isolated — the edge rules plus childlessness, the premise of everything below; eHj is Hj plus isolated vertices, whence (I4a) and (I4b). 

Base case j = 0 . eH0 = G has no fixed vertices and red is the identity. (I1)’s constancy clause is vacuous but its positivity clause is the hypothesis p > 0; (I4) holds because the raw and reduced pairs coincide; (I2) is clause (i) of Proposition 2.1; (I3) follows from Lemma A.3 directly, its constant-extension part not being needed since the pairs coincide. 

Inductive step: choice of the vertex. Fix r := wj+1 ∈ F( eHj ) and write Dr for its district. By (I4a)–(I4b), Dr is also the district of r in Hj and r ∈ F(Hj ). Fixability, de Hj (r) ∩ Dr = {r},says that no other vertex of Dr is a descendant of r; hence r is sterile among the random vertices of Hj [Dr] and Dr \ { r} is random-ancestral in that CADMG. This is what makes the margin P

Step 6, continued: district CADMGs. Two graph identities then transport each factor to its district CADMG of Hj+1 : Hj+1 [D] = Hj [D] for D̸ = Dr , and Hj+1 [E] = ( Hj [Dr \{r}])[ E] for every split E. For the first: r / ∈ D, r is not bidirected-adjacent to D, so neither the arrowheads into D nor pa( D) \ D change — r’s outgoing edges survive, and a fixed parent is still a parent. For the second, four clauses: the bidirected edges deleted by fixing are exactly those incident to r, and a reduced CADMG retains only bidirected edges with both endpoints in its random set, so every r ↔ e is absent from both targets alike — the deletion is invisible to both; fixability of r excludes any r → (Dr \ { r}), since de( r) ∩ dis( r) = {r};every remaining internal edge of E and every external-parent arrow into E survives fixing and is retained by both reductions; and the vertices deleted by red are parents of no remaining random vertex, so they change no pa( ·) \ (·).

Step 6, continued: the nesting identity. Here we also use the general nesting identity 

(H[C])[ B] = H[B], valid for any CADMG H with random set R and every B ⊆ C ⊆ R, with no fixability hypothesis — the typing matters, C must lie in the random set (at its uses in this step, that set is Rj or Dr \ { r}): both sides have random set B; both retain exactly the bidi-rected edges with both endpoints in B, a bidirected edge leaving B failing the both-endpoints rule at each stage; both retain every directed edge with arrowhead in B, because H[C] re-tains all directed edges into C ⊇ B; and the fixed sets agree because pa H[C](B) = pa H(B).Fixability of r is still used elsewhere in this step — it is the hypothesis of Propositions 24 and 25, it makes Dr \ { r} random-ancestral in Hj [Dr] so that (RF2) applies, and it excludes 

r → (Dr \ { r}) — but not for the nesting identity.

The induction gives (a) at every stage and (b) at the terminal stage, for every valid se-quence; this is clause (ii) of Proposition 2.1. Because it proves Markov membership for the raw kernel arising from every valid fixing sequence, p satisfies Definition 27 of [13] and is globally nested Markov. Global nested Markovness of p is the hypothesis under which The-orem 31 (order-independence) and Corollary 32 (parent indexing) of that source apply to p;where Theorem 35 is used for a district reduction below, Proposition 29 is applied first, to pass from global nested Markovness to nested factorization. 

Clause (c), reduction by reduction; this is clause (iii) of Proposition 2.1. Fix a reachable 

R0 ⊆ V , its reduced pair (H0, q R0 ), and an intrinsic C ∈ I (H0); fix an admissible Evans– Richardson recursive derivation from H0 to C, and choose a valid RRS fixing sequence σ

implementing it — a derivation does not determine one, the fixing order within a district reduction being free. For a random-ancestral reduction to A, remove the vertices outside A

by successively fixing a childless vertex of the current random graph; such a vertex exists in reverse topological order because no vertex outside A is an ancestor of A, and Proposi-tion 20 of [13] identifies fixing a childless vertex with marginalization, so this agrees with the Evans–Richardson mA reduction. For a district reduction to D, Proposition 18 supplies fixable vertices in the other nonempty districts and Proposition 24 shows that fixing can split the current district but cannot merge districts; with global nested Markovness established, Proposition 29 supplies nested factorization and Theorem 35 identifies the resulting D ker-nel with the D factor of the current reachable kernel, while Proposition 3.6 and Corollary 3.9 of [7] identify that positive factor with the unique recursive dD kernel. Thus both kinds of recursive step agree with fixing. The stepwise-valid fixing constructions concatenate. Theorem 31 of [13] makes the result-ing terminal graph and raw kernel independent of the valid order. Consequently, if 

erC := ϕR0\C {ϕV \R0 (p; G); ϕV \R0 (G)},

then 

erC (xC | xV \C ) = ϕV \C (p; G)( xC | xV \C ).

Three facts, used by the equalities below, make the chain well founded: • the chosen σ, valid for the reduced pair (H0, q R0 ), lifts to the raw constant-extension pair by (I4); • fixing commutes with constant extension followed by reduction, so the two routes through the diagram agree; • the concatenated sequence ω0 ◦ σ makes C a district of a graph reachable from G, hence 

C ∈ I (G) and the original-law intrinsic kernel qC is defined at all. The induction invariant and Corollary 32 then provide the canonical parent-indexed equalities 

red C (erC ) = rC = qC ,

each parent-indexed kernel read as its constant extension in the suppressed arguments; that is, rC = red C ϕσ(qR0 ; H0) = red C ϕω0◦σ(p; G) = red C ϕV \C (p; G) = qC , the third equal-ity by Theorem 31 applied in G. Proving the chain for the chosen σ and then applying order-invariance removes the dependence on the choice, so the conclusion is derivation-level. Hence (2.7) equals (2.3) whenever the CADMG kernel is reached from an ADMG law. No fixing claim is made for a freestanding CADMG; there the recursively derived rC is the operative object. 60 

A.3.4. The ambient recovery map and the inverse identities. For completeness, we con-struct the ambient map rather than attributing it to the literature. Fix a topological order τ , one complete recursive derivation scheme for every intrinsic set of H, and a reference state for every variable. Apply the scheme to an arbitrary positive array z ∈ O H = (0 , ∞)XR×XW . An ancestral reduction is computed by finite summation. At a district reduction, use the selected topological order restricted to the current random set and form the usual conditional-product version. At each such step, write pre τ (v) for the current random vertices preceding v under the restricted order. Then 

rzD := Y

> v∈D

zv|pre τ (v),W , zv|pre τ (v),W := zv, pre τ (v)|W

zpre τ (v)|W

.

Here numerator and denominator denote the corresponding finite margins of the current pos-itive array. Away from the model, a derived array may retain arguments that the target re-duced CADMG does not retain. Whenever this happens, evaluate those extra arguments at their fixed reference states. Repeating these operations reaches one selected array for every intrinsic set; applying the coordinate ratio then defines an element eΘH(z) ∈ UH.Only finitely many sums, products and divisions occur, and every denominator is a posi-tive sum of positive entries. Thus eΘH is a rational, hence C∞, map on the full-dimensional open set OH. On a positive recursively factorizing kernel, Proposition 3.6 and Corollary 3.9 identify the selected conditional products with the unique recursive factors. Consequently, the extra arguments are irrelevant there and eΘH = Θ H on the model. Different derivation schemes or reference states may give different extensions away from the model; neither The-orem 31 of [13] nor any other source is being invoked for off-model sequence invariance. Theorem 5.5 of [7] supplies the sums-and-divisions smoothness observation in binary nota-tion; Appendix C uses the same recovery operations for finite state spaces, and Corollary 5.6 explicitly returns to that general setting. Finally, the construction in the preceding subsections gives ΦH{ΘH(qR)} = qR for every positive recursively factorizing kernel. Conversely, if qR = Φ H(η) > 0 is a probability kernel, clause (i) makes it recursively factorizing and clause (ii) makes its representing coordinate unique. Since η is one representation, eΘH(qR) = η. These are exactly the two identities in (2.8), completing the proof.

PROOF OF THEOREM 3.1. Step 1: openness, normalization, and model membership. By Remark 2.2, each cell of ΦH(η) is a polynomial in η. Hence ΩH is the finite intersection, over 

XR × XW , of inverse images of (0 , ∞) under continuous maps, and is open. Corollary 2.4 gives ΦH(η) ∈ K H for every η ∈ UH. If η ∈ ΩH, this array is strictly positive; Proposi-tion 2.5(i) then makes it recursively factorizing. Thus ΦH(η) ∈ Q +(H).

Step 2: inverse identities and bijectivity. For q ∈ Q +(H), Proposition 2.5(iii) defines 

ΘH(q), and clause (v) gives (A.7) ΦH{ΘH(q)} = q. 62 

Since q > 0, this identity also puts ΘH(q) in ΩH by definition; hence it establishes surjec-tivity onto Q+(H). Conversely, Step 1 permits the second identity in clause (v) to be applied to every η ∈ ΩH, yielding 

eΘH{ΦH(η)} = Θ H{ΦH(η)} = η. 

The second identity establishes injectivity, and therefore the map is bijective. On Q+(H)

the recovered vector is unique by clause (ii), so the restriction of the selected ambient exten-sion agrees with ΘH and is independent of the off-model choices. No such independence is asserted elsewhere on OH.

Step 3: ambient smooth left inverse, injective differential, and embedding. Remark 2.2 makes the forward map polynomial and therefore C∞. Proposition 2.5(iv) makes the selected map eΘH : OH → UH rational and C∞. Together with its injective differential, this proves that it is a smooth embedding into KH. Its inverse is the restriction of a smooth ambient map, as asserted.

Constraint-based certificate. The projection can be confirmed without differentiating the chart. Put v := (2 W − 1)(2 B − 1) . A.4.4. Proofs in Section 5. 

SOURCE OF THE IMPORT FOR PROPOSITION 5.1. This proposition assembles the cited identification results for the three intervention classes; it does not infer one intervention class from another. 

Case I1: node interventions. Theorem 48 of [13] gives the fixing-kernel product for a node intervention and makes (5.2) the identification criterion. With the node value aw substituted for each excluded parent w, its district factors are exactly those in (5.3). Applied with target set RI, which is ancestrally closed in GV \AI , the same theorem identifies the joint response law on RI, licensing the joint reading of (5.3) in this case. 

Case I2: complete-source edge interventions. Theorem 1 and equation (13) of [19], applied with target set RI, give the joint response law on RI displayed in (5.3). The application with target set RI is licensed by the ancestral identity an GV \AI (RI) = RI, so the theorem’s pruned set is RI itself. The theorem’s preamble hypotheses are used exactly as stated in (I2) and (H2)–(H4): identification of the node-intervention law a 7 → p({V \ AI}(a)) by that paper’s equation (11), equivalently intrinsicness in G of every district of GV \AI , together with the complete-source convention and the common assignment aa,D to all arrows from source a

into district D ∈ D (HI). The cited formula returns the raw fixing kernels ϕV \D(p; G); these are the constant extensions of the reduced kernels qD,P in their childless fixed arguments (2.1), so boundary evaluation agrees after the irrelevant arguments are suppressed. The sep-arately stated condition (5.2) is retained because the statistical bridge of Theorem 5.5 uses it directly; in the system-wide case Y = V \ AI, where HI = GV \AI , the two conditions coin-cide. Theorem 5 and Corollary 2 of that paper give the corresponding completeness statement within its causal model and intervention convention. 

Case I3: path interventions. For the direct route of (I3), Theorems 3–4 of [16] give the district product and its observed-data identification for the two-value path-specific interven-tion of the direct route in (I3), under the proper, live and consistent path set of (H4) and the no-recanting-district and identifiable-district-term hypotheses of (H3), and within the non-parametric structural equation model with independent errors of their Supplement A.2, which (H2) names. Those results identify the response of Y ; the unsummed product is read only as the normalized auxiliary law supplied by Theorem 5.5, and no joint counterfactual law on 

RI is imported in this case. By the reduced route of (I3), a path intervention with a proper, 

Y -live, Y -consistent path set and an edge-consistent assignment, as required in (H4), enters this case only after an independently established induced-edge equality has reduced it to an intervention satisfying every condition in (I2), to which Case I2 then applies; Section 5.3, Lemma 5.7 and Corollary 5.2 of [20] supply the definition, the equality and the identification under the multiple-world model on a DAG, and no general observed-ADMG reduction is inferred from them. Two separate facts make the boundary configuration cI 

> D

well defined with only the two displayed cases: ancestrality in (5.1) forces every non-source parent of an active district into 

RI, and the class-specific assignment — the node value in (I1), the complete-source com-mon value in (I2), and the path-formula value in (I3) — makes every parent in AI assigned rather than left natural. Together they leave no natural parent outside RI; the “natural-parent closure” needed by the compact product is therefore a consequence here, not a condition attributed to Theorem 1 of [19]. In all three cases, the latent-projection and applicable causal-model hypotheses connect the cited causal response to the observed fixing kernels, and (5.2) ensures that each displayed district kernel is identified. Summing over RI \ Y gives the Y margin in every case. No cited theorem is applied outside its stated causal model, intervention class, or hypothesis set, and in (I3) only the Y -margin conclusion is imported. PROOF OF LEMMA 5.3. Step 1: realize the global fixing factor. The hypothesis (5.2) makes D an intrinsic, hence reachable, set of G. Lemma 3.5 therefore writes the left-hand fixing kernel before boundary evaluation as 

red D{ϕV \D(pθ; G)} = Φ G[D](θ↓D).

Step 2: match the graphical indices. The CADMGs G[D] and dD(H) have the same ran-dom vertices and exactly the same directed and bidirected edges among them. Consequently, they have the same intrinsic subsets of D and the same recursive heads. Equation (5.4) says that the active tail of each such intrinsic set is obtained from its global tail by removing exactly the excluded vertices in A.

Step 3: substitute the configured tails. Evaluate each removed tail coordinate at its assigned value aw,D . District compatibility makes this value unambiguous for every coordinate in the 

D factor. By (5.5), the resulting coordinate entry is precisely the corresponding row of Sel I θ.Thus every coordinate appearing in the global D factor is replaced by, and only by, its selected active-chart coordinate. 

Step 4: compare the factors. Insert the entries from Step 3 in the general forward formula (2.4). The two finite sums have identical subsets, signs, recursive-head partitions, and coor-dinate products, so they agree term by term. This proves (5.6). Steps 2–4 are algebraic and do not use positivity. Positivity is used only now: θ ∈ ΩG and Lemma 3.5 make the evaluated fixing kernel strictly positive for every fixed-parent configuration; equality transfers that pos-itivity to the active factor. Thus the identity uses active intrinsicness and district-compatible tail values, while strict positivity additionally uses θ ∈ ΩG ; it uses no causal-model hypothe-sis. PROOF OF COROLLARY 5.4. Apply Lemma 5.3 once at the specified active district D. Its right-hand side uses only selected coordinates indexed by intrinsic sets S ⊆ D. Every such 

S has native tag δ(S) = δ(D), whereas u ∈ U nat  

> K

has nonzero entries only at tags with native district K. If K̸ = δ(D), all active-D rows of Sel I u are therefore zero. Differentiating the right-hand side of (5.6) gives zero, and the equality gives the asserted derivative on the left. No causal identification hypothesis is used in this locality argument. PROOF OF THEOREM 5.5. Step 1: reassemble every configured district factor. For each 

D ∈ D (H), Lemma 5.3 replaces the configured global fixing kernel by its selected active factor. Multiplying those identities gives 

ΓI(pθ)( xR) = Y

Step 3: enter the active positivity domain. Every factor in Step 1 is strictly positive by Lemma 5.3; hence their product, equivalently the active forward array in Step 2, is strictly positive in every cell and fixed slice. By the defining equivalence (2.9), this is exactly the assertion Sel I θ ∈ ΩH.

The statistical proof uses θ ∈ ΩG for positivity, (5.2) to realize every active district factor, and the fixed, district-compatible boundary values to define the tail slices. It does not use the latent causal model or any causal identification theorem; those hypotheses enter only Proposition 5.1 at the true law. PROOF OF COROLLARY 5.6. Equation (5.9) gives P I ∈ N +(H). PROOF OF COROLLARY 6.1. Equation (6.1) identifies the EIF as the tangent-space pro-jection of eϕP . The internal orthogonal sum in Theorem 3.8 and the district projection formula (3.8) therefore give the displayed sum. If a native district carries several active derivative channels, linearity of the derivative first adds their coordinate derivative vectors in that dis-trict; the single inverse Gram matrix is then applied to the aggregate. PROOF OF PROPOSITION 6.2. Step 1: compact positive neighborhood. Because ΩG is open, a sufficiently small closed Euclidean ball K about θ0 is convex and satisfies K ⋐ ΩG .Each cell map θ 7 → pθ(xV ) is polynomial and strictly positive on K; compactness and finite-ness of XV therefore give one constant c > 0 for the first bound in (6.3).

Step 4: updated chart rate. If M = sup θ∈K 1 ∥ζ(θ)∥, then 

∥θ⋆ − θ0∥ ≤ ∥ bθ − θ0∥ + M |bϵ|,

which proves (6.25) and keeps the update inside K1 with probability tending to one. 

Step 5: exact von Mises identity and its two remainders. By (6.23) and the definition of 

R2,

F (θ⋆) − F (θ0) = ( Pn − P )ϕθ⋆ + R2(θ⋆, θ 0).ORDER-FREE INFERENCE IN NESTED MARKOV MODELS 77 

The convexity of K1 licenses Lemma 6.4, so the last term is op(n−1/2). Fixed finite support and the Lipschitz line of (6.3) give 

|(Pn − P )( ϕθ⋆ − ϕθ0 )| ≤ ∥ Pn − P ∥1∥ϕθ⋆ − ϕθ0 ∥∞ = op(n−1/2).

Step 6: first-order limit and variance consistency. Substitution into the von Mises identity of Step 5 gives 

F (θ⋆) − F (θ0) = ( Pn − P )ϕθ0 + op(n−1/2),

which proves (6.26); the ordinary i.i.d. finite-support central limit theorem supplies its Gaus-sian limit. Moreover, ∥ϕθ⋆ − ϕθ0 ∥∞ = op(1) and the finite-support law of large numbers prove variance consistency. 

Step 7: one-step equivalence. Corollary 6.10, formed from the same initial bθ, has the identical leading expansion. Subtracting the two expansions proves (6.27). All rate and convergence-in-probability conclusions extend to the stated fallback, because their failure probabilities increase by at most the probability of Ecn, which tends to zero. The fallback remains in K0 ⋐ ΩG ; it need not solve the empirical EIF equation. PROOF OF COROLLARY 6.17. Step 1: model membership. On En, Theorem 6.14 keeps every point of the local update in ΩG . On Ecn, the fallback in Theorem 6.16 belongs to K0 ⋐

ΩG . Thus pθ⋆ ∈ N +(G) and F (θ⋆) is the target functional at a single model law on every sample. 

Step 2: response-range preservation. For a fixed intervention of the classes (I1)–(I3), 

F (θ⋆) is the expectation of g under the positive normalized configured active law supplied by Theorem 5.5. For an admissible independent-draw mixture, it is a convex combination of such expectations. In either case a ≤ g ≤ b implies a ≤ F (θ⋆) ≤ b.A.4.6. Proofs in Section 7.

Suppose the stated observational order exists. Contracting each literal block to one vertex preserves the order of every observational arrow between blocks and every added source-precedence arrow. The induced block order is therefore topological, so the block graph is acyclic. 

Step 2: sufficiency. Suppose the block graph is acyclic and choose one of its topological or-ders. Within each active district choose a topological order of the induced directed subgraph; every source block is already a singleton. Concatenate these within-block orders in the block order. An internal observational arrow is respected by the within-block order, a cross-block observational arrow is respected by the block order, and an added arrow {a} → D places 

a before every vertex of D. The concatenated order is therefore the required observational topological order, with every block a literal interval. The necessity direction uses the displayed observational order; the sufficiency direction uses acyclicity of the block graph and of the directed part of the ADMG, together with the block construction in (7.3). APPENDIX B: EXACT WORKED EXAMPLES AND COMPUTATIONAL VERIFICATION This appendix records the exact laws, certificates and computational checks behind the worked examples of Sections 2–8. Each subsection groups the graphs, laws and certificates of one example or control; the reproducing scripts, the evidence classification and the secondary diagnostics of the finite-sample study are collected in Section B.8. 

B.1. The primary example. The binary worked example uses V = {W, A, B, Z, Y }

with the graph in (4.1). Two rational laws are used: the primary law P in (B.1) and the second law P ′ in (B.2), in which all three latent pathways are active; Table 4 summarizes the exact checks performed at both. 

The nested model of (4.1) is its ordinary Markov model. Let M+ := {p > 0 : W ⊥⊥

(A, B ), Y ⊥⊥ W | (B, Z )} on an arbitrary finite state space. Then N+(G) = M+. Nested membership gives both independences through the global Markov property (Proposi-tion 2.1(ii) and [13, Theorem 16]); both are m-separations of G: every path from W to 

A or to B collides at Z or at Y , and given {B, Z } every path from W to Y is blocked at Z,at B, or at the collider A. Conversely, let p ∈ M + and write p = p(w) q∆(a, b, z, y | w) with 

q∆ := p(· | w), a strictly positive kernel on the single-district CADMG G[∆] . Since G[∆] has one dis-trict, only (RF2) is at issue; every proper ancestral set of G[∆] is an ancestral set of G[ABZ ]

or of G[BZY ] with the same margin and, by the nesting identity (H[C])[ B] = H[B], the same target CADMG, so it suffices to treat these two. (i) P

Thus its district quotient is a directed three-cycle although its directed vertex graph is acyclic; see Figure 3. For P ∈ N +(G3), define the raw residuals (B.11) RA = A′ − EP (A′ | C′), RB = B − EP (B | A), RC = C − EP (C | B′).

Their pairwise orthogonality is structural. Every P ∈ N +(G3) recursively factorizes by Proposition 2.1(i) and therefore lies in the Markov class Pc(G3) by Lemma A.3; its m-separation global Markov property [13, Theorem 16] gives 

B ⊥⊥ (A′, C ′) | A, C ⊥⊥ (A, B ) | B′, A′ ⊥⊥ (C, B ′) | C′.

For example, 

EP (RARB ) = E P

RA EP {RB | A, A ′, C ′} = 0 ,

and, in the same pattern, EP (RB RC ) = E P [RB EP {RC | A, B, B ′}] = 0 by the second in-dependence, while EP (RARC ) = E P [RC EP {RA | C, B ′, C ′}] = 0 by the third. Hence (B.12) EP (RARB ) = E P (RARC ) = E P (RB RC ) = 0 

for every P ∈ N +(G3).These zeros do not certify tangency. Let P3 be the strictly positive rational law specified by (B.15) below, and evaluate the residuals above at P = P3. Exact metric projection gives (B.13)

Thus this graph supplies a structurally forced false positive for tangency: the graph-implied conditional independences together with observational centering force the three displayed pairwise inner products to vanish even though the directions are not model scores. Non-tangency and the nonzero third moment are law-level counterexamples; unlike the pairwise zeros, they are not asserted as universal graph identities. The independently generated second law reported later in this appendix is a computational replication only, not part of this formal counterexample. 90

B.4. Mixed-cardinality controls. Two exact latent-SEM laws exercise the general corner-state convention rather than its binary specialization: the graph of Section 4 with ternary Z (48 cells, nine intrinsic sets, chart dimension 38) and the cyclic-quotient graph with ternary A′ (24 cells, six intrinsic sets, chart dimension 14). Both verifications check roundtrip at all cells, tangent nullity and forward rank ( 38 and 14 ), and the Gram counts col-lected in the table of Section B.2; both scripts also assert algebraic normalization and district factorization at seeded pseudorandom signed rational coordinate vectors (three and two per script, respectively) and retain a 4000-point signed-factor search only as a negative control, not as proof of the domain product; the universal identities are Corollary 2.4 and Lemma 3.4. B.5. Intervention identification and independent-draw mixtures. This subsection records the two latent-variable models behind Example 5.2, the compatible-edge interven-tion control for the configured product of Proposition 5.1, and the exact checks of the independent-draw mixture identities of Section 5. 

The mixed-boundary construction of Example 5.2. On A → B, A → B′, B ↔ B′, let A ∼

Bernoulli( p), U ∼ Bernoulli(1 /2) , and E, E ′ ∼ Bernoulli(1 /10) be mutually independent, where 0 < p < 1. With ⊕ denoting addition modulo two, consider Model 1: B = U ⊕ E, B′ = U ⊕ A ⊕ E′,

Model 2: B = U ⊕ A ⊕ E, B ′ = U ⊕ E′.

In either model, conditional on A = a, B is uniform and B ⊕ B′ = a ⊕ E ⊕ E′. Since P( E ⊕

E′ = 0) = (9 /10) 2 + (1 /10) 2 = 41 /50 , the conditional joint table in both models is 

P( B = b, B ′ = b′ | A = a) = 

(

41 /100 , b ⊕ b′ = a, 

9/100 , b ⊕ b′̸ = a. 

Multiplication by the common marginal law of A proves equality of the two observed laws, and every observed cell is positive. For the joint law P{B(0) = b, B ′(1) = b′ | A = 1 }

of the split responses of Example 5.2, the counterfactual indexed by the mixed bound-ary (aA→B , a A→B′ ) = (0 , 1) at the natural value A = 1 , the two model parities become 

1 ⊕ E ⊕ E′ and E ⊕ E′: the first law assigns total mass 9/50 to the two equal cells and 

41 /50 to the two unequal cells, the second reverses those totals, and the mass within each parity class is split equally, so the two mixed-boundary kernels are at total-variation distance

Thus ∥K1(1) − K2(1) ∥TV = 16 /25 for the natural-retention responses Ki(a) of Exam-ple 5.2. At the natural value A = 0 the mixed boundary is (0 , 0) and the two kernels coincide, so K1(0) = K2(0) ; the difference of the natural-retention laws averaged over 

A ∼ Bernoulli( p) is p{K1(1) − K2(1) }, of total-variation distance 16 p/ 25 > 0, and the same value is the distance of the joint (A, B, B ′) response laws. In the zero-noise specializa-tion the mixed-boundary kernels are at distance one and the unconditional response laws at distance p; the noisy version shows that the obstruction persists inside the positive model. 

Intervention-bridge controls. On the graph A → B → C, A → B′, and B ↔ B′, an ex-act latent-SEM verification compares the compatible edge intervention assigning A = 0 to both arrows entering {B, B ′} with the configured fixing product. Equality holds at all eight active cells at the base law and three independently perturbed SEM mechanisms. Three ad-ditional unrestricted positive chart perturbations verify the statistical-neighborhood identity, positivity, and normalization without presuming a latent realization. At every checked law, the configured product equals the explicitly instantiated active-graph chart at the selected tail coordinates, and active-chart extraction recovers those coordinates exactly. All 80 cross-native factor derivatives and all eight active-law derivatives along the excluded source block vanish. Both an exact dual-number calculation and an independent five-point polynomial calculation verify (5.13), with native contributions −67 /30 and −271 /280 . The complete observed score basis then yields an EIF with zero mean satisfying all nine coordinate Riesz equations; all 20 cross-native Gram entries vanish exactly. The incompatibility verification covers both the zero-noise and the positive-noise witness of Example 5.2; for the positive-noise witness, the mixed-boundary distance 16 /25 and the unconditional split-response distance 16 p/ 25 are derived above. 92 

Independent-draw mixture controls. On the binary graph of Section 4 with source W , the global and active charts have dimensions 24 and 15. At a latent-SEM law and two unrestricted positive-chart perturbations, the verification instantiates both fixed W responses using literal 

15 × 24 0 /1 selection matrices. It verifies fixed-component normalization and active-chart recovery, and at the base law also matches both components to direct latent-SEM interven-tions at all 32 active cells. For the marginally standardized independent redraw, the complete EIF has zero mean and satisfies all 24 coordinate Riesz equations; deleting the weight term fails exactly the singleton {W } equation. The component and weight terms occupy comple-mentary native blocks in this root-singleton-source setting, all 23 cross-native Gram entries vanish, the exogenous-mixture EIF equals the corresponding average of the fixed-component EIFs, and a point mass recovers the fixed construction. A non-marginal smooth law νθ(1) = 1 /5 + P θ(Y = 1) /2 separately verifies the general 

Dμ formula and all 24 Riesz equations. Its weight derivative is nonzero on the source coor-dinate and eight active coordinates, overlapping the component derivative on all eight. As a named negative-control invariant, the deliberately incorrect marginal-standardization weight formula remains exactly mean zero but fails nine of the 24 Riesz equations at the base law and both perturbations; the nine failed coordinates are exactly the support of the true cross-block weight derivative. A scalar, dual-number-free exact symmetric-difference calculation independently verifies all 24 derivatives for each of the standardized and cross-block targets. 

B.6. Estimated EIF, targeting, and source-block drift. 

Estimated-EIF controls. The estimated-EIF verification retains the same chart but adds the smooth nonpolynomial rule 

ρθ(1) = 1 + P θ(Y = 1) 3 + 2P θ(Y = 1) , ρθ(0) = 1 − ρθ(1) .

Exact algebra verifies its derivative and certifies nonpolynomiality along a fixed affine co-ordinate line. At the base law and four successively halved rational chart perturbations, every observational score is centered and every native Gram block has positive exact LDL ⊤ pivots. An independent scalar affine-primitive calculation agrees with all 48 coordinate derivatives across the standardized and ρ targets. The score basis, Gram blocks, component contribution, weight contribution, Gram coef-ficients, and EIF all change at first order, while the coherent population one-step remain-der scales quadratically for both policies. Omitting either contribution, using the wrong marginal weight for ρ, or dropping fitted-law centering leaves a first-order drift. The two distinct bilinear terms in the target-value and weight-coordinate expansions are nonzero and scale quadratically. Finally, the component, weight, and Gram perturbations satisfy the exact inverse-matrix coefficient identity and the centered modular population-drift identity. 

Targeting controls. The targeting verification deliberately separates exact finite algebra from numerical flow evidence. At the base law of Section 4 and two unrestricted positive-chart perturbations, for both a fixed response and the smooth rational-mixture target, the rational suite asserts cellwise ϕθ = JPθ ζ(θ), DF (θ)[ ζ(θ)] = Pθϕ2 

> θ

, and directional normal-ization. It also asserts the exact null update at a synthetic score-equation law, the fixed-direction score-discrepancy identity and its first-order scaling, quadratic central-difference convergence to the population score slope −P ϕ 2, and fixed point-mass examples in which the coherent one-step estimate exits [0 , 1] while the corresponding plug-in target remains in range. A separate 70 -digit implementation integrates the fixed-response least-favorable coordi-nate ODE with a named fixed-step classical RK4 solver. All selected stages and endpoints ORDER-FREE INFERENCE IN NESTED MARKOV MODELS 93 

remain positive and normalized, and ϕ = Jζ is reassembled there as an indexing check; the Riesz coordinate equations provide the substantive gradient check. The 2/4/8/16 -step end-point refinements have fourth-order ratios 16 .003659 and 16 .001830 . The checked local score root has residual below 5.2 × 10 −34 , a strict sign change, and negative curvature; at a score-equation fit the numerical stopping rule returns the null update, whose exact algebraic validity is established by the rational suite. Along four halved perturbations, the targeted plug-in mi-nus coherent one-step differences have ratios between 3.9985 and 4.0000 , and every targeted indicator mean remains in [0 , 1] . The observed 18 nonzero active coordinates are law and boundary specific; only vanishing of the excluded source block is treated as structural. These deterministic log values are checked by corresponding assertions. 

Source-block drift controls. The exact source-drift suite uses the same signed remainder as (6.6). It asserts R2(η, θ 0) = 0 for 60 nontrivial binary source-block changes and for 16 simultaneous two-coordinate changes of a ternary source block, including positive marginals close to the boundary. In every case it separately checks that the fixed-response means and conditional observed law given the source remain unchanged, the active EIF contribution has zero truth expectation, and the source contribution equals ψW − F (η) pointwise and 

F (θ0) − F (η) in truth expectation. The suite also retains the boundaries of the proposition. Twelve multicoordinate active-block changes have nonzero exact remainders of both signs, while eight special single-coordinate active lines have zero remainder by coordinatewise affinity; hence neither arbi-trary outcome-side robustness nor a converse is inferred. Ten source-only changes under the smooth non-marginal law ρ(1) = {1 + P θ(Y = 1) }/{3 + 2 P θ(Y = 1) } have nonzero re-mainder. Finally, the exact targeting field has nonzero active coordinates at a source-only fit, and a rational forward step leaves the source-only slice with nonzero remainder. This is exact finite-law evidence for Proposition 6.5, together with a negative control against promoting that proposition to an exact universal-flow TMLE theorem.

In summary, at Ptwo indicator-basis enumeration proves prefix transport and every residual-centering identity exactly; both sides of (7.12) equal −259 /6000 under the state-dependent both-wrong specification; all four correctness patterns cancel; the three constant-offset controls give −1/10 , −1/5, −3/10 ; and the sequential representer satisfies all 15 chart-coordinate Riesz equations but has exact projection gap 740027 /559138125 .The mediation-graph count mentioned in Remark 7.9 is as follows, with Kmed the num-ber of causally ordered mediators of the generalized mediation functional of [27]. For ori-entation only, and under Section 7’s system-wide fixed-node representation: let Gmed be a directed acyclic graph whose vertex set is exactly {X, A, M 1, . . . , M Kmed , Y }, one vertex per displayed block, and take Hmed to be its vertex-induced subgraph on every vertex but A.A directed acyclic graph carries no bidirected edge, so every district of Gmed is a single-ton and source isolation (7.1) holds. Taking AD = {A} when A is a parent of the vertex of D, and AD = ∅ otherwise, gives source-tail coverage, since A is the only excluded ver-tex; each added precedence arrow then duplicates an existing observational arrow, so the block graph (7.3) is Gmed itself and is acyclic. Assumption 7.2 is thus satisfied here rather than merely posited. Deleting A leaves Kmed + 2 active singleton districts, so do( A = a)

makes the present reading report at most 2Kmed +2 choice-vector labels. Three scope condi-tions are doing work and none may be dropped: the guarantee is for a single interventional mean E{Y (a)}, which is one component of a total-effect contrast — consistency of the con-trast E{Y (a)} − E{Y (a′)} needs simultaneous correctness for both components and is not proved here; additional vertices increase the district count, and Section 5’s ancestry-pruned active graph GRI can decrease it; and a multivariate mediator or baseline represented by sev-eral vertices contributes further singleton districts. Assumption 7.2 is in force throughout. 

The strict variance gap of Proposition 7.8. Proposition 7.8 uses the rational law in (B.1). Exact enumeration reproduces the constants established in the proof of Proposition 7.8 in Section A.4.6; the raw sequential representer satisfies all 24 Riesz equations and its projection residual is orthogonal to the canonical gradient. 

The one-edge Verma variant of Remark 7.10. Let G+ be the graph of (4.1) with the addi-tional directed edge W → B. B.8. Evidence status and reproducibility. 

Evidence status at a glance. Numerical diagnostics in this appendix are reported at the precision displayed and are reproducible with the supplied scripts; exact rational certificates, prescribed algorithm constants and stated bounds are not covered by this convention. The grid below is a nonfloating reading aid; the suite descriptions in Sections B.1–B.7 record what each suite asserts, and the evidence distinction that closes this section states what each class of computation establishes. > exact rational, fully specified in this appendix Remark 7.10 Cyclic and three-district controls exact; independently written replications are corroboration only Sections 4.4–4.5, Sections B.2–B.3 Intervention-bridge controls exact, including the 16 /25 and 16 p/ 25

Reproducibility artifacts. The aforementioned suites’ scripts, deterministic logs, and repro-duction instructions are arranged by suite in the supplemental material and can be obtained by formal email upon request. 

Finite-sample study: implementation settings. The executable structural checks of the finite-sample implementation govern complete inclusion, targeting convergence, positivity, and range preservation, but deliberately do not turn Monte Carlo coverage into a pass/fail crite-rion. The preliminary table is ¯pn(x) = {Nx + 1 /2}/(n + 16) , adding a Jeffreys pseudocount 

1/2 to each of the 32 cells. Its extracted coordinate is accepted only when every recon-structed cell exceeds 10 −8; otherwise a 55-step bisection locates a locally probed boundary point of the segment from the uniform 1/32 reference law, after which the segment fraction is multiplied by 0.98 and the accepted coordinate is verified strictly positive. The extracted coordinate satisfies the rate condition of Corollary 6.10: at the strictly interior truth, whose minimum cell 27 /8000 exceeds the acceptance floor, the smoothed empirical table is √n-consistent and the ambient recovery map eΘG is smooth near the true table, so the extracted coordinate is √n-consistent, hence op(n−1/4), and the retraction is inactive with probability tending to one. Each targeting update recomputes the EIF coordinate and performs a bounded numerical likelihood line search over locally probed signed bounds; nonpositive candidates receive in-finite loss and every accepted iterate is verified positive. The two signed bracket radii are capped at 2; a boundary is located using the 10 −10 cell floor, 45 bisection steps, and the same 0.98 contraction. The line search rejects candidate cells at or below 10 −12 . At most 200 updates are attempted. If the positive bracket or line search fails, or the update limit is reached before |Pnϕ| ≤ 10 −7, the last positive iterate is retained and the run is classified as nonconverged. The reported run has no such failures under this 10 −7 rule. On the empty-{W =0 } event, of probability 2−n at this law, the sequential estimator– standard-error pair is set to the prespecified fallback (1 /2, 1/2) : the fallback point estimate lies in the response range, the accompanying standard error is a prespecified convention rather than an influence-function estimate, and the resulting Wald interval 1/2 ± z0.975 /2 is not range-preserving. No such event occurred in any of the 1500 reported replicates; a synthetic all-W =1 sample test asserts exactly this pair, and the fallback branch is outside the executed path of the recorded run. 98 

Finite-sample study: secondary diagnostics. In the study of Section 8, no targeting run failed, left the positive chart, or violated the response range, and the largest terminal empirical EIF residual was below 10 −7. Initial-coordinate retraction occurred in 12 .4% , 1.4% , and 0% 

of replicates at n = 1000 , 2500 , and 5000 , without excluding any sample. The mean abso-lute one-step–targeted difference decreased from 1.85 × 10 −4 to 3.85 × 10 −5 over the three sample sizes. As a secondary diagnostic — interior meaning a minimum fitted cell above 

10 −7 and convergence a score norm below 10 −4 — every interior converged full-chart MLE satisfied the numerical null-update property, with largest observed correction 3.82 × 10 −7;boundary fits were excluded from this diagnostic only, not from the primary results. 

Reproducibility. The complete rational specifications of the two laws of the graph of Sec-tion 4, the variant law P +, the formal three-district law, and both sequential specifications appear above. Thus every law and nuisance input used by the exact claims in Sections 4 and 7 is available in the paper. The accompanying supplemental archive supplies every script named in this appendix, deterministic logs for the exact-arithmetic suites, and concise repro-duction instructions. The main theorems do not rely on a numerical acceptance threshold: all finite-state algebraic checks use exact rational arithmetic, while the separately identified ODE experiment reports its precision and refinement diagnostics explicitly. 

Role of the computations. Analytic arguments establish the general results of this paper. Exact arithmetic verifies the specified finite laws and identities on which the worked exam-ples rest, at the evidence class recorded for each suite in the grid above; the numerical flow experiment and the Monte Carlo study illustrate computational behaviour and are not cited as evidence for any analytic conclusion. Two limits are not carried by a grid row: the binary count dC = 2 |T (C)| alone is not evidence for the precise nonbinary coordinate convention, which the mixed-cardinality calculations of Section B.4 exercise directly but only at two fi-nite examples; and the cyclic two- and three-district calculations are sharp falsification tests for quotient-order arguments, not replacements for the arbitrary-graph proof of top-level pa-rameter separation. 

B.9. Finite-sample study: implementation and results. This subsection records the implementation and the complete results of the finite-sample illustration of Section 8, at the rational law of Proposition 7.8. Section 8 of the article states the design—target, sample sizes, replicates, seed and the three estimators—and reports the variance comparison; the exact variance constants are established in the proof of that proposition, in Section A.4.6. 

The sequential estimator as a same-sample pooled ratio. With ben := Pn1(W = 0) the full-sample empirical propensity, the weights 1(Wi = 0) /ben have empirical mean exactly one on the positive-denominator event {ben > 0}, so the augmented average Pn[ bQ0 + 1(W =0) {Y − bQ0}/ben] takes the same value for every constant bQ0, namely the pooled ratio 

bψseq =

Pni=1 1(Wi = 0) Yi

Pni=1 1(Wi = 0) .

This ratio is the implementation used here; it is not the cross-fitted estimator (7.13), which does not equal it in general. By the delta method for a ratio of means, its influence function at 

P0 is 1(W = 0)( Y − ψ0)/PP0 (W = 0) = ϕseq , the all-correct sequential influence function in Proposition 7.8, so the variance comparison below applies to it. 

Standard errors. Each estimator is accompanied by a nominal 95% Wald interval whose standard error is computed from its estimated influence function, matching the implementa-tion exactly:

Initialization. Both chart estimators start from a preliminary chart coordinate obtained by recursive-head extraction from the Jeffreys-smoothed empirical 32 -cell table ¯pn(x) = 

{Nx + 1 /2}/(n + 16) , retracted towards the uniform reference law only when its forward reconstruction has a cell at or below the acceptance floor; this preliminary estimator satisfies the rate condition of Corollary 6.10, and the justification, the retraction rule, and the search settings are recorded in Section B.8. 

Practical targeting. The targeted estimator performs a bounded numerical likelihood line search along a straight chart direction, recomputes the EIF coordinate at the updated law, and iterates until |Pnϕ| ≤ 10 −7, every accepted iterate being verified strictly positive; it is the model-valid practical variant of Remark 6.19, not a numerical claim about the exact flow of Theorem 6.14. 

Uncertainty and the exceptional event. For the sequential estimator the plugged-in influ-ence values have empirical mean exactly zero on the positive-denominator event, so the cen-tered and uncentered standard errors coincide. On the event that no sampled observation has 

Wi = 0 , of probability 2−n at this law, the sequential ratio is undefined and is replaced by the prespecified fallback recorded in Section B.8; the event occurred in none of the 1500 

replicates.

B.10. Three shortcuts that fail: the exact laws. This section carries in full the three examples summarized in Section 7.1 of the article: each exhibits, at an exact strictly positive 100 

law, the failure of one natural shortcut in the restricted sequential construction, and together they motivate the scope conditions stated there. The first shortcut deletes earlier source values from the histories of later propensities. The product of the resulting inverse propensities need not have mean one, so it need not define a change of probability. For example, let A1 ≺ A2 be binary and suppose 

P( A1 = 1) = 12 , P( A2 = 1 | A1 = 1) = 45 , P( A2 = 1 | A1 = 0) = 15 .

Then P( A2 = 1) = 1 /2. At the anchor (1 , 1) , using the two marginal denominators gives 

E

 1(A1 = 1) 1/2

1(A2 = 1) 1/2



= 85 ,

whereas keeping A1 = 1 in the second denominator replaces 1/2 by 4/5 and makes the expectation one. The second shortcut allows an intervention source inside a transition block by restricting the order only among active vertices. Consider 

L ↔ Y, L → A → Y, 

under do( A = 1) . The active graph has the single district {L, Y }, so the literal block-order condition is vacuous after restricting to the active vertices. Nevertheless every observational topological order has L ≺ A ≺ Y : the source lies inside the active block. A candidate weight may then depend on L, part of the current transition, and the true district residual need not be centered against that weight. The exact positive law and nuisance specification in (B.16)– (B.17) of Section B.7 exhibit a correct outcome residual and a weight of this form whose drift is 9/160 , rather than zero. The third shortcut substitutes conditioning for fixing at a source that is bidirected-confounded with its district, even when the source precedes the block. In the graph A ↔ Y ,fixing A under a node intervention gives the kernel qY (y) = p(y), whereas conditioning on 

A gives p(y | a). At the strictly positive law 

p(0 , 0) = p(1 , 1) = 25 , p(0 , 1) = p(1 , 0) = 110 ,

the two probabilities of Y = 1 are 1/2 and 4/5, respectively, so the ordinary weight 

1(A = 1) /P( A = 1) transports to the wrong law. Placing the source before the block does not exclude this example: what removes A from a bidirected district is the fixing divisor p(a | y),not a propensity measurable with respect to the past. 

B.11. A strictly positive mixture that is not Markov. This section carries in full the counterexample summarized in the independent-draw extension of Section 5 of the article: an equal mixture of two strictly positive component laws that is not Markov for its active graph, which is why the active chart is used componentwise there rather than assigned to the mixture. For example, let A → X and A → Z, with binary X, Z and no edge between them in the active graph. Suppose that under do( A = 0) the two responses are independent 

Bernoulli(1 /4) variables and that under do( A = 1) they are independent Bernoulli(3 /4) 

variables. Both fixed laws are strictly positive and Markov for the two isolated active ver-tices. Their equal mixture, Qmix say, satisfies 

PQmix (X = 1 , Z = 1) = 516 ̸ = 14 = P Qmix (X = 1) P Qmix (Z = 1) ,

so it is not Markov, and hence not nested Markov, for that active graph. ORDER-FREE INFERENCE IN NESTED MARKOV MODELS 101
